They investigated the conditions under which polyharmonic functions are unique and the types of boundary conditions that can be imposed to ensure well-posed problems.
In this section our goal is to provide an integral representation for axially polyharmonic functions in the Clifford algebra setting.
Precisely, this class of functions is defined as follows.

Definition 4.1

(Axially polyharmonic function)
.

Let
k\geq 2
be an integer. Let
U
be an open set in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. We say that a function
f:U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\to\mathbb{R}_{n}
, of class
\mathcal{C}^{2k}
is left (resp. right) axially polyharmonic function (of degree
k
) if it is of axial type, see (
3.1
), and if

\Delta^{k}_{n+1}f(x)=0,\qquad\forall x\in U.

The definition above will be used in consideration of the Fueter-Sce mapping theorem. Now, we give some particular example of polyharmonic functions.

Example 4.2

(Axially harmonic polynomials)
.

An example of axially harmonic functions, i.e. polyharmonic functions of degree
1
,
is given by the following polynomials

(4.1)

\mathcal{H}_{k}^{n}(x):=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\mathcal{T}_{\ell}^{k}(n)x^{k-\ell}\bar{x}^{\ell},\qquad\mathcal{T}_{\ell}^{k}(n):=\binom{k}{\ell}\frac{\left(\frac{n-1}{2}\right)_{k-\ell}\left(\frac{n-1}{2}\right)_{\ell}}{(n-1)_{k}},\ \ \ x\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}.

An interesting property of these polynomials is that

(4.2)

\mathcal{H}_{k}^{n}(x_{0})=x_{0}^{k}

since
\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\mathcal{T}_{\ell}^{k}(n)=1
, see
[
58
]
.

Example 4.3

(Axially polyharmonic polynomials)
.

A second example of axially polyharmonic polynomials of degree
k
, first introduced in
[
17
]
, is given by

(4.3)

H_{k}(x):=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}x^{k-\ell}\overline{x}^{\ell},\ \ \ x\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}.

We show that through an appropriate factorization of the second map in the Fueter-Sce extension theorem, the class of left (or right) axially polyharmonic functions emerges naturally within the Fueter-Sce framework.

Remark 4.4
.

Throughout this section, we will prove each result only for left slice hyperholomorphic functions, as the corresponding result for right slice hyperholomorphic functions can be derived using similar arguments.

Our goal now is to determine a suitable factorization of the Fueter-Sce map to obtain axially polyharmonic functions. First, however, we need some preliminary results on the spherical decomposition of the Laplace operator in
n+1
variables raised to an appropriate power and on the Dirac operator
D
. These results will be crucial for the remainder of the section.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be an axially symmetric open set that intersects the real line and
let
f\in\mathcal{SH}_{L}(U)
. Then,
for any
(u,0)\in U\cap\mathbb{R}
there exists a ball
B(u,r)\subseteq U
, centred in
u
and radius
r>0
,
where for any
x=u+Iv\in B(u,r)

the functions
\alpha(u,v)
and
\beta(u,v)
can be written as

\alpha(u,v)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}v^{2j}}{(2j)!}\partial_{u}^{2j}[f(u)],\qquad\beta(u,v)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}v^{2j+1}}{(2j+1)!}\partial_{u}^{2j+1}[f(u)],

where
f(u)=\alpha(u,0)
.

Proof.

Since, by assumption,
f
is a slice hyperholomorphic function on an axially symmetric slice domain
U
, for any
a\in U\cap\mathbb{R}
there exists a ball
B(a,r)\subseteq U
where for any
x\in B(a,r)
we have

f(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(x-a)^{k}}{k!}\partial_{S}^{k}f(a)

where
\partial_{S}
is the left slice derivative of
f
(see
[
51
]
or
[
41
, Def. 2.1.10 , Eq. (2.15)]
) and it is given by
\partial_{S}^{k}f(a)=\partial_{u}^{k}f(a)
.
Now we choose
a=u
so in the ball
B(u,r)\subseteq U
for any
x=u+Iv
such that

(u,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
u+\mathbb{S}v\subset U
,
and for
v
small enough we have

f(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(x-u)^{k}}{k!}\partial_{u}^{k}f(u)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(Iv)^{k}}{k!}\partial_{u}^{k}f(u)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}v^{2j}}{(2j)!}\partial_{u}^{2j}f(u)+I\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}v^{2j+1}}{(2j+1)!}\partial_{u}^{2j+1}f(u),

so, recalling that
f(x)=\alpha(u,v)+I\beta(u,v)
, from the last equality, we deduce the series expansions of
\alpha(u,v)
and
\beta(u,v)
.
∎

Remark 4.6
.

Let
f
be a slice hyperholomorphic function of the form
f(x)=\alpha(u,v)+I\beta(u,v)
. Then
f(u)=\alpha(u,0)+I\beta(u,0)
. Due to the even-odd conditions, see (
2.3
),we have
f(u)=\alpha(u,0)
.

The following result was originally proved in
[
85
]
in a more general setting, and later addressed in the context of Clifford algebras in
[
67
, Thm. 11.33]
. We include the proof here for completeness

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
.
Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be an axially symmetric open set that intersects the real line and
let
f\in\mathcal{SH}_{L}(U)
.
Then, for
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

(4.4)

\Delta^{m}_{n+1}f(x)=2^{m}(h_{n}-m+1)_{m}\left[\left(\frac{1}{v}\partial_{v}\right)^{m}\alpha(u,v)+I\left(\partial_{v}\frac{1}{v}\right)^{m}\beta(u,v)\right],

where
(h_{n}-m+1)_{m}
is the Pochhammer symbol and recall that
f(x)=\alpha(u,v)+I\beta(u,v)
for

x=u+Iv
.

Proof.

We prove the result by induction on
m
. We start proving the case
m=1
. By
[
81
,
55
]
we know that if
f(x)=\alpha(u,v)+I\beta(u,v)
then we can write

(4.5)

Df(x)=\left(\partial_{u}\alpha(u,v)-\partial_{v}\beta(u,v)-\frac{2h_{n}}{v}\beta(u,v)\right)+I\left(\partial_{u}\beta(u,v)+\partial_{v}\alpha(u,v)\right),

and

(4.6)

\overline{D}f(x)=\left(\partial_{u}\alpha(u,v)+\partial_{v}\beta(u,v)+\frac{2h_{n}}{v}\beta(u,v)\right)+I\left(\partial_{u}\beta(u,v)-\partial_{v}\alpha(u,v)\right).

By (
4.5
) and (
4.6
) we have

(4.7)

\displaystyle\Delta_{n+1}f(x)=D\overline{D}f(x)

\displaystyle=\left(\partial_{u}^{2}\alpha(u,v)+\partial_{v}^{2}\alpha(u,v)+\frac{2h_{n}}{v}\partial_{v}\alpha(u,v)\right)+I\left(\partial_{u}^{2}\beta(u,v)+\partial_{v}^{2}\beta(u,v)+\partial_{v}\left(\frac{2h_{n}}{v}\beta(u,v)\right)\right).

Now since the function
f
is left slice hyperholomorphic, by (
2.4
) we have

(4.8)

\partial_{u}^{2}\alpha(u,v)=-\partial_{v}^{2}\alpha(u,v),\qquad\partial_{u}^{2}\beta(u,v)=-\partial_{v}^{2}\beta(u,v).

Thus by plugging (
4.8
) into (
4.7
) we have

\Delta_{n+1}f(x)=2h_{n}\left(\frac{1}{v}\partial_{v}\alpha(u,v)+I\partial_{v}\left(\frac{1}{v}\beta(u,v)\right)\right).

This proves the result for
m=1
. In order to show the result for all
m
we define
\alpha_{0}(u,v)=\alpha(u,v)
and
\beta_{0}(u,v)=\beta(u,v)
and for
m=1,2,...

(4.9)

\alpha_{m}(u,v):=2^{m}m!\left(\frac{1}{v}\partial_{v}\right)^{m}\alpha(u,v),\quad\beta_{m}(u,v):=2^{m}m!\left(\partial_{v}\frac{1}{v}\right)^{m}\beta(u,v).

By
[
50
,
85
]
we know that

(4.10)

\partial_{u}\alpha_{m}(u,v)=\partial_{v}\beta_{m}(u,v)+2m\frac{\beta_{m}(u,v)}{v},\qquad\partial_{v}\alpha_{m}(u,v)=-\partial_{u}\beta_{m}(u,v).

By using the notaions in (
4.9
) we have to prove that

(4.11)

\Delta_{n+1}^{m}f(x)=\frac{(h_{n}-m+1)_{m}}{m!}(\alpha_{m}(u,v)+I\beta_{m}(u,v)).

We suppose the statement true for
m-1
and we prove for
m
. By the inductive hypothesis and (
4.7
) we have

(4.12)

\displaystyle\Delta_{n+1}^{m}f(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(h_{n}-m+2)_{m-1}}{(m-1)!}\Delta_{m+1}\left(\alpha_{m-1}(u,v)+I\beta_{m-1}(u,v)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(h_{n}-m+2)_{m-1}}{(m-1)!}\left[\left(\partial_{u}^{2}\alpha_{m-1}(u,v)+\partial_{v}^{2}\alpha_{m-1}(u,v)+\frac{2h_{n}}{v}\partial_{v}\alpha_{m-1}(u,v)\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+I\left(\partial_{u}^{2}\beta_{m-1}(u,v)+\partial_{v}^{2}\beta_{m-1}(u,v)+2h_{n}\partial_{v}\left(\frac{\alpha_{m-1}(u,v)}{v}\right)\right)\right].

By (
4.10
) we deduce that

(4.13)

\partial_{u}^{2}\alpha_{m}(u,v)=-\partial_{v}^{2}\alpha_{m-1}(u,v)-\frac{2(m-1)}{v}\partial_{v}\alpha_{m-1}(u,v)

and

(4.14)

\partial_{u}^{2}\beta_{m-1}(u,v)=-\partial_{v}^{2}\beta_{m-1}(u,v)-2(m-1)\partial_{v}\left(\frac{\beta_{m-1}(u,v)}{v}\right).

Now, we plug (
4.13
) and (
4.14
) into (
4.12
) and we get

(4.15)

\Delta_{n+1}^{m}f(x)=\frac{2(h_{n}-m+2)_{m-1}(h_{n}-m+1)}{(m-1)!}\left[\left(\frac{1}{v}\partial_{v}\alpha_{m-1}(u,v)\right)+I\partial_{v}\left(\frac{\beta_{m-1}(u,v)}{v}\right)\right].

Now, we observe that

(4.16)

(h_{n}-m+2)_{m-1}(h_{n}-m+1)=(h_{n}-m+1)_{m},

and

(4.17)

\frac{2}{(m-1)!}\left(\frac{1}{v}\partial_{v}\alpha_{m-1}(u,v)\right)=2^{m}\left(\frac{1}{v}\partial_{v}\right)^{m}\alpha(u,v)=\frac{\alpha_{m}(u,v)}{m!},

(4.18)

\frac{2}{(m-1)!}\partial_{v}\left(\frac{\beta_{m-1}(u,v)}{v}\right)=2^{m}\left(\partial_{v}\frac{1}{v}\right)^{m}\beta(u,v)=\frac{\beta_{m}(u,v)}{m!}.

Thus by plugging (
4.16
), (
4.17
) and (
4.18
) into (
4.15
) we get (
4.11
).

∎

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
m
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
, such that
j\geq m
. Then, we have

(4.19)

\left(\frac{1}{v}\partial_{v}\right)^{m}v^{2j}=\frac{2^{m}j!}{(j-m)!}v^{2j-2m},

and

(4.20)

\left(\partial_{v}\frac{1}{v}\right)^{m}v^{2j+1}=\frac{2^{m}j!}{(j-m)!}v^{2j-2m+1}.

Proof.

We proceed by induction to prove equation (
4.19
). For
m=1
we have

\left(\frac{1}{v}\partial_{v}\right)v^{2j}=2j\frac{1}{v}v^{2j-1}=2\frac{j!}{(j-1)!}v^{2j-2}.

We suppose the equation (
4.19
) holds for
m
and we want to prove it for
m+1
. Thus we have

\begin{split}\left(\frac{1}{v}\partial_{v}\right)^{m+1}v^{2j}&=\left(\frac{1}{v}\partial_{v}\right)\left(\frac{1}{v}\partial_{v}\right)^{m}v^{2j}=\left(\frac{1}{v}\partial_{v}\right)\left(2^{m}\frac{j!}{(j-m)!}v^{2j-2m}\right)\\
&=2^{m}\frac{j!}{(j-m)!}2(j-m)v^{2j-2m-2}=2^{m+1}\frac{j!}{(j-(m+1))!}v^{2j-2(m+1)}.\end{split}

For the proof of equation (
4.20
) we proceed by induction in a similar way. For
m=1
we have

\left(\partial_{v}\frac{1}{v}\right)v^{2j+1}=\partial_{v}v^{2j}=2jv^{2j-1}=2\frac{j!}{(j-1)!}v^{2j-1}.

We suppose the equation (
4.20
) holds for
m
and we want to prove it for
m+1
. Thus we have

\begin{split}\left(\partial_{v}\frac{1}{v}\right)^{m+1}v^{2j+1}&=\left(\partial_{v}\frac{1}{v}\right)\left(\partial_{v}\frac{1}{v}\right)^{m}v^{2j+1}=\left(\partial_{v}\frac{1}{v}\right)\left(2^{m}\frac{j!}{(j-m)!}v^{2j-2m+1}\right)\\
&=2^{m}\frac{j!}{(j-m)!}2(j-m)v^{2j-2m-1}=2^{m+1}\frac{j!}{(j-(m+1))!}v^{2j-2(m+1)+1}.\end{split}

∎

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
.
Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be an axially symmetric open set that intersects the real line and
let
f\in\mathcal{SH}_{L}(U)
.
Then, for
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

(4.21)

\displaystyle D\Delta_{n+1}^{m}f(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{m}(h_{n}-m+1)_{m}\left[\left(\frac{1}{v}\partial_{v}\right)^{m}\partial_{v}\beta(u,v)-\partial_{v}\left(\partial_{v}\frac{1}{v}\right)^{m}\beta(u,v)+\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\frac{2h_{n}}{v}\left(\partial_{v}\frac{1}{v}\right)^{m}\beta(u,v)\right],

where
f(x)=\alpha(u,v)+I\beta(u,v)
for any
x=u+Iv
such that

(u,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
u+\mathbb{S}v\subset U
.

Proof.

By combining (
4.4
) and (
4.5
) we get

\displaystyle D\Delta_{n+1}^{m}f(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{m}(h_{n}-m+1)_{m}\left\{\left[\partial_{u}\left(\frac{1}{v}\partial_{v}\right)^{m}\alpha(u,v)-\partial_{v}\left(\partial_{v}\frac{1}{v}\right)^{m}\beta(u,v)\right.\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left.-\frac{2h_{n}}{v}\left(\partial_{v}\frac{1}{v}\right)^{m}\beta(u,v)\right]\right.\left.+I\left[\partial_{u}\left(\partial_{v}\frac{1}{v}\right)^{m}\beta(u,v)+\partial_{v}\left(\frac{1}{v}\partial_{v}\right)^{m}\alpha(u,v)\right]\right\}.

Now, since the function
f
is slice hyperholomorphic, by the Cauchy-Riemann equation (
2.4
), we have

(4.22)

\displaystyle D\Delta_{n+1}^{m}f(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{m}(h_{n}-m+1)_{m}\left\{\left[\left(\frac{1}{v}\partial_{v}\right)^{m}\partial_{v}\beta(u,v)-\partial_{v}\left(\partial_{v}\frac{1}{v}\right)^{m}\beta(u,v)\right.\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left.-\frac{2h_{n}}{v}\left(\partial_{v}\frac{1}{v}\right)^{m}\beta(u,v)\right]\right.\left.+I\left[-\left(\partial_{v}\frac{1}{v}\right)^{m}\partial_{v}\alpha(u,v)+\partial_{v}\left(\frac{1}{v}\partial_{v}\right)^{m}\alpha(u,v)\right]\right\}.

Since

\left(\partial_{v}\frac{1}{v}\right)^{m}\partial_{v}\alpha(u,v)=\partial_{v}\left(\frac{1}{v}\partial_{v}\right)^{m}\alpha(u,v),

from (
4.22
) we get (
4.21
).
∎

Now, we have all the tools to show that axially polyharmonic function arise naturally in the Fueter-Sce construction.

Theorem 4.10
.

Let
n
be an odd number, set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
and assume that
\ell
is an integer such that
1\leq\ell\leq h_{n}
.
Then, the application of the following operator

(4.23)

{T}_{FS}^{(I)}=D\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}.

on a left (resp. right) slice hyperholomorphic function defined on an axially symmetric open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, we obtain a left (resp. right) axially polyharmonic of degree
h_{n}-\ell+1
.

Proof.

Let
f
be a left slice hyperholomorphic function. The fact that
T_{FS}^{(I)}f(x)
is of axial form follows by Proposition
4.9
. Now, we need to show that

\Delta_{n+1}^{h_{n}-\ell+1}T_{FS}^{(I)}f(x)=0,\qquad\forall x\in U.

By the Fueter-Sce theorem, see Theorem
3.9
, and the fact that
\Delta_{n+1}=D\overline{D}
we obtain

\displaystyle\Delta_{n+1}^{h_{n}-\ell+1}T_{FS}^{(I)}f(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta_{n+1}^{h_{n}-\ell+1}D\Delta_{n+1}^{\ell-1}f(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta_{n+1}^{h_{n}}Df(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D\Delta_{n+1}^{h_{n}}f(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

∎

The result above leads to the following notion.

Definition 4.11

(Axially polyharmonic functions associated with
\mathcal{SH}_{L}(U)
and
\mathcal{SH}_{R}(U)
)
.

Let
U
be an open set. Let
n
be an odd number, set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
and consider any integer
\ell
such that
1\leq\ell\leq h_{n}
.
We denote the set of left axially polyharmonic functions of degree
h_{n}-\ell+1
as

\mathcal{APH}^{L}_{h_{n}-\ell+1}(U)=\{D\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}f\ :\ f\in\mathcal{SH}_{L}(U)\},

and the class of right axially polyharmonic functions of degree
h_{n}-\ell+1
as

\mathcal{APH}^{R}_{h_{n}-\ell+1}(U)=\{f\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}D\ :\ f\in\mathcal{SH}_{R}(U)\}.

As a consequence of Theorem
4.10
we have the function spaces of the axially polyharmonic functions fine structure.

Corollary 4.12
.

Let
U
be an open set. Let
n
be an odd number, set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
and assume that
\ell
is an integer such that
1\leq\ell\leq h_{n}
.
Then, we have the following factorization of the Fueter-Sce construction:

(4.24)

\begin{CD}&&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{SH}_{L}(U)}@>{\ \ D\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}}>{}>{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{APH}^{L}_{h_{n}-\ell+1}(U)}@>{\ \ \overline{D}\Delta_{n+1}^{h_{n}-\ell}}>{}>{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{AM}_{L}(U)}.\end{CD}

Moreover, the factorization (
4.24
) can be further expanded as follows:

(4.25)

\begin{CD}&&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{SH}_{L}(U)}@>{\ \ D\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}}>{}>{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{APH}^{L}_{h_{n}-\ell+1}(U)}@>{\ \ \Delta_{n+1}}>{}>{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{APH}^{L}_{h_{n}-\ell}(U)}@>{\ \ \Delta_{n+1}}>{}>{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{APH}^{L}_{h_{n}-\ell-1}(U)}\\
&&@>{\ \ \Delta_{n+1}}>{}>{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}...}@>{\ \ \Delta_{n+1}}>{}>{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{APH}^{L}_{1}(U)}@>{\ \ \overline{D}}>{}>{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{AM}_{L}(U)}.\end{CD}

Proof.

It is a direct consequence of Theorem
4.10
.
∎

In order to define functions of operators within the context of axially polyharmonic functions, it is crucial to obtain an integral representation of these functions.
The key step is to apply the operator
T_{FS}^{(I)}
to the left (or right) slice hyperholomorphic Cauchy kernel in its second form. To achieve this, we first need to establish some preliminary results.

Proposition 4.13
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
. For
s
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, such that
s\notin[x]
, and
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
we have

(4.26)

D((s-\overline{x})\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-m})=2(m-h_{n}-1)\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-m}

and

(4.27)

\overline{D}(\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-m})=2m(s-\overline{x})\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-m-1},

where
Dirac operator
D
and its conjugate
\overline{D}
are defined in (
1.1
) and

\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-1}
is defined in (
2.8
).

Proof.

In order to prove (
4.26
) we have to observe that

\partial_{x_{0}}((s-\overline{x})\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-m})=-\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-m}-m(s-\overline{x})\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-m-1}(-2s+2x_{0})

and

\partial_{x_{i}}((s-\overline{x})\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-m})=e_{i}\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-m}-m(s-\overline{x})\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-m-1}(x)(2x_{i}).

Thus we obtain

\begin{split}D((s-\overline{x})\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-m})&=\partial_{x_{0}}((s-\overline{x})\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-m})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}\partial_{x_{i}}((s-\overline{x})\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-m})\\
&=(-1-n)\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-m}+2m((s-\overline{x})s-x(s-\overline{x}))\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-m-1}\\
&=(-1-n)\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-m}+2m(\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x))\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-m-1}\\
&=(-1-n+2m)\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-m}\\
&=2(m-h_{n}-1)\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-m}\end{split}

and this gives formula (
4.26
). Now we prove formula (
4.27
) observing that

\partial_{x_{0}}(\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-m})=2m(s-x_{0})\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-m-1}

and

\partial_{x_{i}}(\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-m})=-2mx_{i}\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-m-1}.

Thus we have

\overline{D}(\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-m})=\partial_{x_{0}}(\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-m})-\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}\partial_{x_{i}}(\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-m})=2m(s-\overline{x})\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-m-1}

and this concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.14
.

In the following we will use the Pochhammer symbol defined as

(x)_{\ell}=\frac{\Gamma(x+\ell)}{\Gamma(x)}.

When we choose
x=-h_{n}
, where
h_{n}
is the Sce exponent and
where
\ell
is an integer such that
0\leq\ell\leq h_{n}
.
In order to avoid the poles of the Gamma function in the points
0,-1,-2,\ldots

the symbol
(-h_{n})_{\ell}
has to be interpreted as

(-h_{n})_{\ell}=(-1)^{\ell}(h_{n}-\ell+1)_{\ell}=(-1)^{\ell}\frac{\Gamma(h_{n}+1)}{\Gamma(h_{n}-\ell+1)}

which excludes the poles of the function
\Gamma
.

For the sake of completeness, we give a short proof of Theorem 3.3 in
[
54
]
that we will use in the sequel.

Proposition 4.15
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
. For
s
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
such that
s\notin[x]
,
and for any integer
\ell
such that
0\leq\ell\leq h_{n}
we have

(4.28)

\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}(S^{-1}_{L}(s,x))=4^{\ell}\ell!(-h_{n})_{\ell}(s-\overline{x})\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-\ell-1},

and

\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}(S^{-1}_{R}(s,x))=4^{\ell}\ell!(-h_{n})_{\ell}(s-\overline{x})\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-\ell-1},

where
\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-1}
is defined in (
2.8
).

Proof.

First we prove (
4.28
) by induction with respect to the index
\ell
. When
\ell=1
, applying (
4.26
) and (
4.27
) we have

\displaystyle\Delta_{n+1}(S^{-1}_{L}(s,x))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{D}(D(S^{-1}_{L}(s,x)))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2h_{n}\overline{D}(\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-4h_{n}(s-\overline{x})\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-2}.

Assuming formula (
4.28
) holds to be true for
\ell
, we will prove it for
\ell+1
. By the induction principle, Proposition
4.13
and the definition of the slice monogenic Cauchy kernel we have

\begin{split}\Delta^{\ell+1}_{n+1}(S^{-1}_{L}(s,x))&=\overline{D}D(\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}(S^{-1}_{L}(s,x)))\\
&=4^{\ell}\ell!(-h_{n})_{\ell}\overline{D}D((s-\overline{x})\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-\ell-1})\\
&=2\cdot 4^{\ell}\ell!(-h_{n})_{\ell}\overline{D}\left((\ell-h_{n})\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-\ell-1}\right)\\
&=4^{\ell+1}\ell!(\ell+1)(-h_{n})_{\ell}(\ell-h_{n})(s-\overline{x})\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-\ell-2}\\
&=4^{\ell+1}(\ell+1)!(-h_{n})_{\ell+1}(s-\overline{x})\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-\ell-2},\end{split}

that concludes the proof.
∎

Now, we have all the tools to apply the operator
{T}_{FS}^{(I)}
,
i.e
D\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}
, to the second form of the slice hyperholomorphic Cauchy kernel
S^{-1}_{L}(s,x)
.

Theorem 4.16
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
. The, for any
s
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
with
s\notin[x]
, and for any integer
\ell
such that
1\leq\ell\leq h_{n}
, we have

(4.29)

D\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}(S^{-1}_{L}(s,x))=2^{2\ell-1}(\ell-1)!(-h_{n})_{\ell}\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-\ell},

and

(S^{-1}_{R}(s,x))D\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}=2^{2\ell-1}(\ell-1)!(-h_{n})_{\ell}\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-\ell}

Proof.

By formula (
4.26
), Proposition
4.15
, and the properties of the Pochhammer symbol
(-h_{n})_{\ell-1}
we have

\begin{split}D(\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}(S^{-1}_{L}(s,x)))&=4^{\ell-1}(\ell-1)!(-h_{n})_{\ell-1}D((s-\overline{x})\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-\ell})\\
&=2^{2\ell-1}(\ell-1)!(-h_{n})_{\ell-1}(\ell-h_{n}-1)\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-\ell}\\
&=2^{2\ell-1}(\ell-1)!(-h_{n})_{\ell}\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-\ell}.\end{split}

∎

Remark 4.17
.

If we consider
\ell=1
and
n=3
in Theorem
4.16
we re-obtain
[
32
, Theorem 4.1]
.

Definition 4.18

(The kernel
\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,x)
)
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
. Then, for any
s
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
with
s\notin[x]
,
and for any integer
\ell
such that
1\leq\ell\leq h_{n}
, we define function

(4.30)

\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,x):=\sigma_{n,\ell}\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-\ell},

and we set

(4.31)

\sigma_{n,\ell}:=2^{2\ell-1}(\ell-1)!(-h_{n})_{\ell}.

Proposition 4.19
.

Let
n
be a fixed odd number and
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
. For
s
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
such that
s\notin[x]
and
1\leq\ell\leq h_{n}
we have that the function
\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,x)
, defined in (
4.30
), is intrinsic slice hyperholomorphic in
s
and axially left and right polyharmonic in
x
of degree
h_{n}-\ell+1
.

Proof.

Since the function
\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-1}
is intrinsic slice hyperholomorphic we get that
\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-\ell}
is intrinsic slice hyperholomorphic as well.
Thus the function
\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,x)
is intrinsic slice hyperholomorphic in the variable
s
for
s\notin[x]
.
Now, we show that the function
\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,x)
is of axial type. By
[
37
, Thm. 7.3.1]
we deduce that

\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,x)=\sigma_{n,\ell}\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-\ell}=\sigma_{n,\ell}\mathcal{Q}^{-h_{n}}_{c,s}(x)\mathcal{Q}^{h_{n}-\ell}_{c,s}(x)=\frac{\sigma_{n,\ell}}{\gamma_{n}}[F_{n}^{L}(s,x)s-xF_{n}^{L}(s,x)]\mathcal{Q}^{h_{n}-\ell}_{c,s}(x),

where
\gamma_{n}
is defined in (
3.12
).
By Proposition
3.16
we know that the left
F
-kernel is a function of axial type, so we can write

\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,x)=A_{1}(x_{0},|\underline{x}|)+\underline{\omega}B_{1}(x_{0},|\underline{x}|),

where
A_{1}(x_{0},|\underline{x}|)
and
B_{1}(x_{0},|\underline{x}|)
are suitable functions.
Finally the fact that the function
\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,x)
is left axially polyharmonic in the variable
x
follows by Theorem
4.10
and by Theorem
4.16
.
∎

Now, our goal is to give a series expansion of the axially polyharmonic function
\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,x)
. To do this we need some preliminary results.

In
[
56
]
the authors have already studied the application of the operator
D\Delta^{h_{n}-1}_{n+1}
to the monomial
x^{k}
.

Theorem 4.20
.

Let
n
be a fixed odd number and
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
. For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
and
k\geq 2h_{n}-1
we have

D\Delta_{n+1}^{h_{n}-1}x^{k}=\frac{\gamma_{n}}{(2h_{n})!}\frac{k!}{(k-2h_{n}+1)!}\mathcal{H}_{k-2h_{n}+1}^{n}(x)

where
\mathcal{H}_{k-2h_{n}+1}^{n}(x)
are defined in (
4.1
).

Now, we prove a relation between the coefficients of the Clifford-Appell polynomials, see (
3.4
), and the coefficients of the axially harmonic polynomials in (
4.1
).

Lemma 4.21
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
. For
\ell=0,1,\ldots,k

let

\mathcal{C}_{\ell}^{k}(n)
be the coefficient of the homogeneous polynomials of degree
k
, given by (
3.4
)
and let
\mathcal{T}_{\ell}^{k}(n)
be
the coefficients axially harmonic polynomials,
given by (
4.1
). Then we have the following relations.

(1)

For
m\in\mathbb{N}
, with
m-2h_{n}+1\geq 0
, we have

\mathcal{C}^{m-2h_{n}+1}_{m-2h_{n}+1}(2h_{n}+1)\binom{m+1}{m+1-2h_{n}}=\binom{m}{2h_{n}-1}\mathcal{T}^{m-2h_{n}+1}_{m-2h_{n}+1}(2h_{n}+1).

(2)

For
m\in\mathbb{N}
and for
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that
0\leq\ell\leq m-2h_{n}
, with

m-2h_{n}\geq 0
, we have

\mathcal{C}^{m-2h_{n}+1}_{\ell}(2h_{n}+1)\binom{m+1}{m+1-2h_{n}}-\mathcal{C}^{m-2h}_{\ell}(2h_{n}+1)\binom{m}{m-2h_{n}}=\binom{m}{2h_{n}-1}\mathcal{T}^{m-2h_{n}+1}_{\ell}(2h_{n}+1).

Proof.

We prove point (1) recalling that by definition of the coefficients
\mathcal{C}_{\ell}^{k}(n)
in
(
3.4
)

\mathcal{C}_{\ell}^{k}(n):=\binom{k}{\ell}\frac{\left(\frac{n+1}{2}\right)_{k-\ell}\left(\frac{n-1}{2}\right)_{\ell}}{(n)_{k}}\ \ \ {\rm for}\ \ \ \ell=0,1,\ldots,k,\ \ \ {\rm and}\ \ \ n\ {\rm odd\ number},

setting
\ell=k=m-2h_{n}+1
, we have

(4.32)

\begin{split}\mathcal{C}^{m-2h_{n}+1}_{m-2h_{n}+1}(2h_{n}+1)\binom{m+1}{m+1-2h_{n}}&=\frac{(h_{n})_{m-2h_{n}+1}}{(2h_{n}+1)_{m-2h_{n}+1}}\binom{m+1}{m+1-2h_{n}}\\
&=\frac{(h_{n})_{m-2h_{n}+1}}{(m+1)!}(2h_{n})!\frac{(m+1)!}{(2h_{n})!(m+1-2h_{n})!}\\
&=\frac{(h_{n})_{m-2h_{n}+1}}{(m+1-2h_{n})!}.\end{split}

On the other side form considering the coefficients

\mathcal{T}_{\ell}^{k}(n):=\binom{k}{\ell}\frac{\left(\frac{n-1}{2}\right)_{k-\ell}\left(\frac{n-1}{2}\right)_{\ell}}{(n-1)_{k}}\ \ \ {\rm for}\ \ \ \ell=0,1,\ldots k,\ \ \ {\rm and}\ \ \ n\ {\rm odd\ number}

given by (
4.1
) and setting
\ell=k=m-2h_{n}+1

we have

(4.33)

\begin{split}\binom{m}{2h_{n}-1}\mathcal{T}^{m-2h_{n}+1}_{m-2h_{n}+1}(2h_{n}+1)&=\frac{m!(h_{n})_{m-2h_{n}+1}}{(2h_{n}-1)!(m-2h_{n}+1)!(2h_{n})_{m-2h_{n}+1}}\\
&=\frac{m!(2h_{n}-1)!(h_{n})_{m-2h_{n}+1}}{(2h_{n}-1)!m!(m-2h_{n}+1)!}\\
&=\frac{(h_{n})_{m-2h_{n}+1}}{(m-2h_{n}+1)!}.\end{split}

The first relation is proved since (
4.32
) and (
4.33
) coincide.

We prove point (2). First we consider the first hand side in the relation in point (2) that contains the coefficients
\mathcal{C}_{\ell}^{k}(n)
so
by (
3.4
) with the restrictions on

m\in\mathbb{N}
and for
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that
0\leq\ell\leq m-2h_{n}
, with

m-2h_{n}\geq 0
, we have

(4.34)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{m-2h_{n}+1}_{\ell}(2h_{n}+1)\binom{m+1}{m+1-2h_{n}}-\mathcal{C}^{m-2h_{n}}_{\ell}(2h_{n}+1)\binom{m}{m-2h_{n}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\binom{m-2h_{n}+1}{\ell}\frac{(h_{n}+1)_{m-2h_{n}+1-\ell}(h_{n})_{\ell}}{(2h_{n}+1)_{m-2h_{n}+1}}\binom{m+1}{m+1-2h_{n}}

\displaystyle-\binom{m-2h_{n}}{\ell}\frac{(h_{n}+1)_{m-2h_{n}-\ell}(h_{n})_{\ell}}{(2h_{n}+1)_{m-2h_{n}}}\binom{m}{m-2h_{n}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(m-2h_{n}+1)!(m-h_{n}+1-\ell)!(h_{n}+\ell-1)!(2h_{n})!(m+1)!}{\ell!(m-2h_{n}+1-\ell)!h_{n}!(h_{n}-1)!(m+1)!(2h_{n})!(m+1-2h_{n})!}

\displaystyle-\frac{(m-2h_{n})!(m-h_{n}-\ell)!(h_{n}+\ell-1)!(2h_{n})!m!}{\ell!(m-2h_{n}-\ell)!h_{n}!(h_{n}-1)!m!(2h_{n})!(m-2h_{n})!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(h_{n}+\ell-1)!(m-h_{n}+1-\ell)!}{\ell!h_{n}!(h_{n}-1)!(m-2h_{n}+1-\ell)!}-\frac{(h_{n}+\ell-1)!(m-h_{n}-\ell)!}{\ell!h_{n}!(h_{n}-1)!(m-2h_{n}-\ell)!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(m-h_{n}-\ell)!(h_{n}+\ell-1)!}{(m-2h_{n}-\ell)!\ell!h_{n}!(h_{n}-1)!}\left[\frac{m-h_{n}+1-\ell}{m-2h_{n}+1-\ell}-1\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(m-h_{n}-\ell)!(h_{n}+\ell-1)!}{(m-2h_{n}+1-\ell)!\ell![(h_{n}-1)!]^{2}}.

On the other side for the
the coefficients
\mathcal{T}_{\ell}^{k}(n)
of axially harmonic polynomials,
given by (
4.1
), we have

(4.35)

\displaystyle\binom{m}{2h_{n}-1}\mathcal{T}^{m-2h_{n}+1}_{\ell}(2h_{n}+1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{m!(h_{n})_{m-2h_{n}+1-\ell}(h_{n})_{\ell}(m-2h_{n}+1)!}{(2h_{n}-1)!(m-2h_{n}+1)!(m-2h_{n}+1-\ell)!\ell!(2h_{n})_{m-2h_{n}+1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{m!(m-h_{n}-\ell)!(h_{n}+\ell-1)!(2h_{n}-1)!}{\ell!(m-2h_{n}+1-\ell)!(2h_{n}-1)!\left[(h_{n}-1)!\right]^{2}m!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(m-h_{n}-\ell)!(h_{n}+\ell-1)!}{\ell!(m-2h_{n}+1-\ell)!\left[(h_{n}-1)!\right]^{2}}.

Since the expressions (
4.34
) and (
4.35
) coincide, we get the result.

∎

Proposition 4.22
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
. For any
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
we have

(4.36)

\Delta^{h_{n}}_{n+1}(x^{1+m})-x\Delta^{h_{n}}_{n+1}(x^{m})=2h_{n}D\Delta^{h_{n}-1}_{n+1}x^{m}.

Proof.

The proof is based on the relation between
of the coefficient of the homogeneous polynomials
P_{k}^{n}(x)
given by (
3.4
)
with
the coefficients axially harmonic polynomials
\mathcal{H}_{k}^{n}(x)

given by (
4.1
).
Applying the second map of the Fueter-Sce mapping theorem, i.e.
T_{FS2}:=\Delta^{h_{n}}_{n+1}
,
to the monomial
x^{m}
(see formula (
3.16
)) and using the first and second relations from Lemma
4.21
we get

\displaystyle\Delta^{h_{n}}_{n+1}(x^{1+m})-x\Delta^{h_{n}}_{n+1}(x^{m})=\gamma_{n}\binom{m+1}{m+1-2h_{n}}P^{2h_{n}+1}_{m+1-2h_{n}}(x)-\gamma_{n}xP^{2h_{n}+1}_{m-2h_{n}}(x)

\displaystyle=\gamma_{n}\binom{1+m}{1+m-2h_{n}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{m-2h_{n}+1}\mathcal{C}^{m-2h_{n}+1}_{\ell}(2h_{n}+1)x^{m+1-2h_{n}-\ell}\bar{x}^{\ell}

\displaystyle-\gamma_{n}\binom{m}{m-2h_{n}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{m-2h_{n}}\mathcal{C}^{m-2h_{n}}_{\ell}(2h_{n}+1)x^{m+1-2h_{n}-\ell}\bar{x}^{\ell}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{n}\sum_{\ell=0}^{m-2h_{n}}\left[\mathcal{C}^{m-2h_{n}+1}_{\ell}(2h_{n}+1)\binom{m+1}{m+1-2h_{n}}-\mathcal{C}^{m-2h_{n}}_{\ell}(2h_{n}+1)\binom{m}{m-2h_{n}}\right]x^{m-2h_{n}-\ell+1}\bar{x}^{\ell}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+\gamma_{n}\binom{m+1}{m+1-2h_{n}}\mathcal{C}^{m-2h_{n}+1}_{m-2h_{n}+1}(2h_{n}+1)\bar{x}^{m-2h_{n}+1}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{n}\frac{m!}{(2h_{n}-1)!(m-2h_{n}+1)!}\Big[\sum_{\ell=0}^{m-2h_{n}}\mathcal{T}_{\ell}^{m-2h_{n}+1}(2h+1)x^{m-2h_{n}-\ell+1}\bar{x}^{\ell}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+\mathcal{T}^{m-2h+1}_{m-2h_{n}+1}(2h_{n}+1)\bar{x}^{m-2h_{n}+1}\Big]

\displaystyle=(2h_{n})\gamma_{n}\frac{m!}{(2h_{n})!(m-2h_{n}+1)!}\sum_{\ell=0}^{m-2h_{n}+1}\mathcal{T}^{m-2h_{n}+1}_{\ell}(2h_{n}+1)x^{m-2h_{n}-\ell+1}\bar{x}^{\ell}

\displaystyle=(2h_{n})D\Delta^{h_{n}-1}_{n+1}x^{m},

where the constant
\gamma_{n}
are given in (
3.12
).
In the last equality we used the application of
D\Delta^{h_{n}-1}_{n+1}
to the monomial
x^{m}
, see Theorem
4.20
.
∎

Now, we have all the tools to give a series expansion of the function
\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,x)
.

Proposition 4.23
.

Let
n
be an odd number, set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2

and assume that
\ell
is an integer such that
1\leq\ell\leq h_{n}
.
Then, for any
x
,
s\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
with
|x|<|s|
,
the function
\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,x)
defined in (
4.30
)
has the series expansion

(4.37)

\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,x)=\sigma_{n,\ell}\sum_{k=2\ell-1}^{\infty}\sum_{\alpha=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\sum_{\beta=0}^{\alpha}K_{k,\alpha,\beta,\ell,h_{n}}\mathcal{H}^{n}_{k-2\alpha+\beta-2\ell+1}(x)(-2x_{0})^{\beta}|x|^{2(\alpha-\beta)}s^{-1-k},

where the axially harmonic polynomials
\mathcal{H}_{k-2\alpha+\beta-2\ell+1}^{n}(x)
are given by (
4.1
),
the constants
K_{k,\alpha,\beta,\ell,h_{n}}
are defined as

(4.38)

K_{k,\alpha,\beta,\ell,h_{n}}:=\binom{2h_{n}-2\alpha+\beta-2\ell+k}{2h_{n}-1}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\beta},

and
\sigma_{n,\ell}
are given in (
4.31
).

Proof.

By Proposition
4.19
we deduce that

(4.39)

\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,x)=\frac{\sigma_{n,\ell}}{\gamma_{n}}[F_{n}^{L}(s,x)s-xF_{n}^{L}(s,x)]\mathcal{Q}^{h_{n}-\ell}_{c,s}(x).

By Proposition
3.17
and (
3.16
) we have

F_{n}^{L}(s,x)=\sum_{k=2h_{n}}^{\infty}\Delta^{h_{n}}_{n+1}(x^{k})s^{-1-k},

thus by Proposition
4.22
, we obtain

(4.40)

\displaystyle F_{n}^{L}(s,x)s-xF_{n}^{L}(s,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=2h_{n}}^{\infty}\Delta_{n+1}^{h_{n}}(x^{k})s^{-k}-x\sum_{k=2h_{n}}^{\infty}\Delta_{n+1}^{h_{n}}(x^{k})s^{-1-k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=2h_{n}-1}^{\infty}\Delta_{n+1}^{h_{n}}(x^{k+1})s^{-1-k}-x\sum_{k=2h_{n}-1}^{\infty}\Delta_{n+1}^{h_{n}}(x^{k})s^{-1-k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2h_{n}\sum_{k=2h_{n}-1}^{\infty}(D\Delta^{h_{n}-1}_{n+1}x^{k})s^{-1-k}.

By (
4.39
), (
4.40
) and the binomial formula, we have

(4.41)

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2h_{n}\sigma_{n,\ell}}{\gamma_{n}}\left(\sum_{k=2h_{n}-1}^{\infty}(D\Delta^{h_{n}-1}_{n+1}x^{k})s^{-1-k}\right)(s^{2}-2x_{0}s+|x|^{2})^{h_{n}-\ell}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2h_{n}\sigma_{n,\ell}}{\gamma_{n}}\left(\sum_{k=2h_{n}-1}^{\infty}(D\Delta^{h_{n}-1}_{n+1}x^{k})s^{-1-k}\right)

\displaystyle\times\left[\sum_{\alpha=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{\alpha}s^{2h_{n}-2\ell-2\alpha}(|x|^{2}-2x_{0}s)^{\alpha}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2h_{n}\sigma_{n,\ell}}{\gamma_{n}}\left(\sum_{k=2h_{n}-1}^{\infty}(D\Delta^{h_{n}-1}_{n+1}x^{k})s^{-1-k}\right)

\displaystyle\times\left[\sum_{\alpha=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\sum_{\beta=0}^{\alpha}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\beta}s^{2h_{n}-2\ell-2\alpha}(-2x_{0}s)^{\beta}|x|^{2\alpha-2\beta}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2h_{n}\sigma_{n,\ell}}{\gamma_{n}}\sum_{\alpha=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\sum_{\beta=0}^{\alpha}\sum_{k=2h_{n}-1}^{\infty}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\beta}(D\Delta^{h_{n}-1}_{n+1}x^{k})s^{-1-k+2h_{n}-2\ell-2\alpha+\beta}

\displaystyle\times(-2x_{0})^{\beta}|x|^{2\alpha-2\beta}.

We change the index
k
in the index
u
setting:
k=u+2h_{n}-2\ell-2\alpha+\beta
. Thus, the previous summation becomes

(4.42)

\begin{split}\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,x)&=\frac{2h_{n}\sigma_{n,\ell}}{\gamma_{n}}\sum_{\alpha=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\sum_{\beta=0}^{\alpha}\sum_{u=2\alpha-\beta+2\ell-1}^{\infty}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\beta}\left(D\Delta^{h_{n}-1}_{n+1}x^{u+2h_{n}-2\ell-2\alpha+\beta}\right)\times\\
&\times s^{-1-u}(-2x_{0})^{\beta}|x|^{2\alpha-2\beta}.\end{split}

If
2\ell-1\leq u<2\alpha-\beta+2\ell-1
, we have that

D\Delta^{h_{n}-1}_{n+1}x^{u+2h_{n}-2\ell-2\alpha+\beta}=0,

due to the fact that

u+2h_{n}-2\ell-2\alpha+\beta\leq 2h_{n}-2

and, thus, in the previous summation the index
u
can start from
2\ell-1
. So by (
4.42
) and Theorem
4.20
we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2h_{n}\frac{\sigma_{n,\ell}}{\gamma_{n}}\sum_{u=2\ell-1}^{\infty}\sum_{\alpha=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\sum_{\beta=0}^{\alpha}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\beta}\left(D\Delta^{h_{n}-1}_{n+1}x^{u+2h_{n}-2\ell-2\alpha+\beta}\right)(-2x_{0})^{\beta}|x|^{2\alpha-2\beta}s^{-1-u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma_{n,\ell}\sum_{u=2\ell-1}^{\infty}\sum_{\alpha=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\sum_{\beta=0}^{\alpha}\binom{2h_{n}-2\alpha+\beta-2\ell+u}{2h_{n}-1}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\beta}

\displaystyle\times\mathcal{H}^{n}_{u-2\alpha+\beta-2\ell+1}(x)(-2x_{0})^{\beta}|x|^{2\alpha-2\beta}s^{-1-u}

This proves the result.

∎

From the previous result, we can determine how the operator
D\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}
acts on the monomial
x^{k}
.

Corollary 4.24
.

Let
n
be an odd number set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
and let

\ell
be an integer such that
1\leq\ell\leq h_{n}
. Then, we have the following facts.
For every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
and
k\geq 2(\ell-1)
, it follows that

(4.43)

D\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}x^{k}=\sigma_{n,\ell}\sum_{\alpha=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\sum_{\beta=0}^{\alpha}K_{k,\alpha,\beta,\ell,h_{n}}\mathcal{H}^{n}_{k-2\alpha+\beta-2\ell+1}(x)(-2x_{0})^{\beta}|x|^{2(\alpha-\beta)},

where
K_{k,\alpha,\beta,\ell,h_{n}}
is defined in (
4.38
), the
axially harmonic polynomials
\mathcal{H}_{k-2\alpha+\beta-2\ell+1}^{n}(x)
are given by (
4.1
) and

\sigma_{n,\ell}
are defined in (
4.31
).

Proof.

By Theorem
4.16
and Proposition
2.9
we have

\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,x)=\Delta_{n+1}^{\ell-1}Dx^{k}=\sum_{k=2\ell-1}^{\infty}\left(D\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}x^{k}\right)s^{-1-k},\qquad|x|<|s|.

Thus, by using the series expansion of the kernel
\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,x)
, see (
4.37
), in the expression above, we obtain (
4.43
) by equating the coefficients of the power series.

∎

Remark 4.25
.

The expression (
4.43
) can be considered the Almansi decomposition of the polyharmonic polynomials
D\Delta_{n+1}^{\ell-1}x^{k}
, see
[
12
, Prop. 1.3]
.

Now, we want to show that the sum of the coefficient of the series (
4.43
) are different from zero.

Proposition 4.26
.

Let
n
be an odd number and
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
.
Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be an axially symmetric open set that intersects the real line and
let
f\in\mathcal{SH}_{L}(U)
.
Then, for
1\leq\ell\leq h_{n}
, we have

(4.44)

\lim_{v\to 0}D\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}f(x)=\frac{4^{\ell}h!(-1)^{\ell}(\ell-1)!}{2(2\ell-1)!(h_{n}-\ell)!}\partial_{u}^{2\ell-1}f(u),

where
f(x)=\alpha(u,v)+I\beta(u,v)
, for any
x=u+Ix
such that
(u,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
f(u)=\alpha(u,0)
.

Proof.

We plug the expansion of
\beta(u,v)
, see Lemma
4.5
, into (
4.21
) (with
m=\ell-1
) and by using the relations in Lemma
4.8
we get

(4.45)

\displaystyle\lim_{v\to 0}\left(\frac{1}{v}\partial_{v}\right)^{\ell-1}\partial_{v}\beta(u,v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{v\to 0}\left(\frac{1}{v}\partial_{v}\right)^{\ell-1}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}}{(2j)!}v^{2j}\partial_{u}^{2j+1}[f(u)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{\ell-1}\lim_{v\to 0}\sum_{j=\ell-1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}j!}{(2j)!(j-\ell+1)!}v^{2j-2(\ell-1)}\partial_{u}^{2j+1}[f(u)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{\ell-1}\frac{(-1)^{\ell-1}(\ell-1)!}{(2\ell-2)!}\partial_{u}^{2\ell-1}[f(u)],

and

(4.46)

\displaystyle\lim_{v\to 0}\partial_{v}\left(\partial_{v}\frac{1}{v}\right)^{\ell-1}\beta(u,v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{\ell-1}\lim_{v\to 0}\sum_{j=\ell-1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}j!(2j-2(\ell-1)+1)}{(2j+1)!(j-\ell+1)!}v^{2j-2(\ell-1)}\partial_{u}^{2j+1}[f(u)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{\ell-1}\frac{(-1)^{\ell-1}(\ell-1)!}{(2\ell-1)!}\partial_{u}^{2\ell-1}[f(u)]

and

(4.47)

\displaystyle 2h_{n}\lim_{v\to 0}\frac{1}{v}\left(\partial_{v}\frac{1}{v}\right)^{\ell-1}\beta(u,v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{\ell}h_{n}\lim_{v\to 0}\sum_{j=\ell-1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}j!}{(2j+1)!(j-\ell+1)!}v^{2j-2(\ell-1)}\partial_{u}^{2j+1}[f(u)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{\ell}h_{n}\frac{(-1)^{\ell-1}(\ell-1)!}{(2\ell-1)!}\partial_{u}^{2\ell-1}[f(u)].

Finally, by putting together (
4.45
), (
4.46
), (
4.47
) and using Proposition
4.9
we obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{v\to 0}D\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}f(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{\ell-1}(h_{n}-\ell+2)_{\ell-1}\left[\frac{2^{\ell-1}(-1)^{\ell-1}(\ell-1)!}{(2\ell-2)!}-\frac{2^{\ell-1}(-1)^{\ell-1}(\ell-1)!}{(2\ell-1)!}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\frac{2^{\ell}h_{n}(-1)^{\ell-1}(\ell-1)!}{(2\ell-1)!}\right]\partial_{u}^{2\ell-1}f(u)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4^{\ell-1}h_{n}!(\ell-1)!(-1)^{\ell-1}}{(h_{n}-\ell+1)!(2\ell-1)!}\left[1-\frac{1}{2\ell-1}-\frac{2h_{n}}{2\ell-1}\right]\partial_{u}^{2\ell-1}f(u)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4^{\ell-1}h_{n}!(\ell-1)!(-1)^{\ell-1}}{(h_{n}-\ell+1)!(2\ell-1)!}\frac{2(\ell-1-h_{n})}{(2\ell-1)}\partial_{u}^{2\ell-1}f(u)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4^{\ell}h_{n}!(-1)^{\ell}(\ell-1)!}{2(2\ell-1)!(h_{n}-\ell)!}\partial_{u}^{2\ell-1}f(u).

∎

Remark 4.27
.

The above results based on Lemma
4.5
, where we assume that

U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
is an axially symmetric open set that intersects the real line,
are of independent interest but are used in this paper only for the following Proposition
4.28
. In the rest of the paper, we relax the assumption that the domain has to intersect the real line.

We now have all the tools to show that the sum of the coefficients in (
4.43
) are different from zero, and actually the sum of the coefficients have closed compact expression.

Proposition 4.28
.

Let
n
be an odd number and let
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
. Then, for
1\leq\ell\leq h_{n}
, we have

\sum_{\alpha=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\sum_{\beta=0}^{\alpha}K_{k,\alpha,\beta,\ell,h_{n}}(-2)^{\beta}=\frac{1}{(2\ell-1)!}\frac{k!}{(k-2\ell+1)!},\qquad k\geq 2\ell-1,

where
K_{k,\alpha,\beta,\ell,h_{n}}
is defined in (
4.38
).

Proof.

We consider in (
4.44
) the function
f(x)=x^{k}
, with
x=u+Iv
and
k\geq 2\ell-1
. Thus we have

(4.48)

D\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}x^{k}|_{\underline{x}=0}=\lim_{v\to 0}D\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}x^{k}=\frac{4^{\ell}h_{n}!(-1)^{\ell}(\ell-1)!}{2(2\ell-1)!(h_{n}-\ell)!}\frac{k!}{(k-2\ell+1)!}x_{0}^{k-2\ell+1}.

If we restrict the expression (
4.43
) to the real, by (
4.2
) we obtain

(4.49)

D\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}x^{k}|_{\underline{x}=0}=\sigma_{n,\ell}\sum_{\alpha=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\sum_{\beta=0}^{\alpha}K_{k,\alpha,\beta,\ell,h_{n}}(-2)^{\beta}x_{0}^{k-2\ell+1}.

By equating the right-hand sides of the relations (
4.48
) and (
4.49
) we get

\sigma_{n,\ell}\sum_{\alpha=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\sum_{\beta=0}^{\alpha}K_{k,\alpha,\beta,\ell,h_{n}}(-2)^{\beta}x_{0}^{k-2\ell+1}=\frac{4^{\ell}h_{n}!(-1)^{\ell}(\ell-1)!}{2(2\ell-1)!(h_{n}-\ell)!}\frac{k!}{(k-2\ell+1)!}x_{0}^{k-2\ell+1}.

Hence, by using the definition of the constant
\sigma_{n,\ell}
, see (
4.31
), we get the result.
∎

Now, we characterize of the kernel of the operator
D\Delta_{n+1}^{\ell-1}
.

Lemma 4.29
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
, with
1\leq\ell\leq h_{n}
. We assume that
U
is a connected slice Cauchy domain and
f\in\mathcal{SH}_{L}(U)
(resp.
f\in\mathcal{SH}_{R}(U)
). Then
f\in\hbox{ker}(D\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1})
if and only if
f(x)=\sum_{\nu=0}^{2\ell-2}x^{\nu}\alpha_{\nu}
(resp.
f(x)=\sum_{\nu=0}^{2\ell-2}\alpha_{\nu}x^{\nu}
), with
\{\alpha_{\nu}\}_{1\leq\nu\leq 2\ell-2}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{n}
.

Proof.

We focus on the case
f\in\mathcal{SH}_{L}(U)
, since for
f\in\mathcal{SH}_{R}(U)
it follows by similar arguments. We proceed by double inclusion.
If we take
f(x)=\sum_{\nu=0}^{2\ell-2}\alpha_{\nu}x^{\nu}
, then it is clear that
f\in\hbox{ker}(D\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1})
. Now, we assume that
f\in\hbox{ker}(D\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1})
. Since the function
f
is left slice hyperholomorphic on a connected slice Cauchy domain, after a change of variable if needed, there exists
r>0
such that the function
f
can be expanded in a convergent series at the origin as follows

f(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}x^{k}\alpha_{k},\quad\hbox{for}\quad\{\alpha_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\subset\mathbb{R}_{n},

for any
x\in B_{r}(0)
. Now, we apply the differential operator
\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}D
and we get

(4.50)

0=\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}Df(x)=\sum_{k=2\ell-1}^{\infty}\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}Dx^{k}\alpha_{k},\qquad\forall x\in B_{r}(0).

We restrict the polynomials
\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}Dx^{k}
to a neighbourhood of the real axis
\Omega
. By (
4.43
) and Proposition
4.28
we have

\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}Dx^{k}|_{\underline{x}=0}=d_{k}x_{0}^{k-2\ell+1},\qquad d_{k}:=\frac{4^{\ell}h_{n}!(-1)^{\ell}(\ell-1)!}{2(2\ell-1)!(h_{n}-\ell)!}\frac{k!}{(k-2\ell+1)!}.

Thus if we restrict (
4.50
) to a neighbourhood of the real axis we have

0=\sum_{k=2\ell-1}^{\infty}d_{k}x_{0}^{k-2\ell+1}\alpha_{k},\qquad\forall x\in B_{r}(0).

Since for
k\geq 2\ell-1
the coefficients
d_{k}
are different from zero we get

\alpha_{k}=0,\qquad k\geq 2\ell-1.

This implies that
f(x)=\sum_{\nu=0}^{2\ell-2}x^{\nu}\alpha_{\nu}
in
\Omega
, and since
U
is connected we get that
f(x)=\sum_{\nu=0}^{2\ell-2}x^{\nu}\alpha_{\nu}
with
x\in U
.

∎

It is also possible to obtain a series expansion of the function
\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,x)
in terms of the polyharmonic polynomials of degree
k
introduced in (
4.3
).

Theorem 4.30
.

Let
n
be an odd number and let
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
. Then, for
s
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
,
such that
|x|<|s|
, the function
\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-1}
defined in (
2.8
), has the series expansion

(4.51)

\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-1}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}H_{k}(x)s^{-2-k}.

Moreover, for
1\leq\ell\leq h_{n}
, the function
\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,x)
defined in (
4.30
), can be written as

(4.52)

\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,x)=\sigma_{n,\ell}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}H_{k}(x)s^{-2-k}\right)^{\ell},

where
H_{k}(x)
are defined in (
4.3
) and constant
\sigma_{n,\ell}
are given in (
4.31
).

Proof.

We start proving the uniform convergence of the series

\mathbf{S}(s,x):=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}H_{k}(x)s^{-2-k}.

By the definition of the polynomials
H_{k}(x)
it is clear that
|H_{k}(x)|\leq(k+1)|x|^{k}.
Then we have

|\mathbf{S}(s,x)|\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}|H_{k}(x)s^{-2-k}|\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(k+1)|x|^{k}|s|^{-2-k}.

Since
|x|<|s|
, by using the ratio test we get that the series
\mathbf{S}(s,x)
is uniformly convergent.
Now, we prove the equality in (
4.51
). By Theorem
4.16
, with
\ell=1
, we have

DS^{-1}_{L}(s,x)=2(-h_{n})_{1}\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-1}.

By using the fact that
D(x^{k})=-2h_{n}H_{k-1}(x)
, see
[
17
, Lemma 1]
and Theorem
2.9
we get

\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-1}=\frac{DS^{-1}_{L}(s,x)}{2(-h_{n})_{1}}=-\frac{1}{2h_{n}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}D(x^{k})s^{-1-k}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}H_{k}(x)s^{-2-k}.

Finally, by Theorem
4.16
and the equality (
4.51
) we get

\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,x)=\sigma_{n,\ell}\left(\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-1}\right)^{\ell}=\sigma_{n,\ell}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}H_{k}(x)s^{-2-k}\right)^{\ell}.

∎

Remark 4.31
.

The convergence of the series in (
4.52
) follows from the fact that is finite product of converging series.

By equating the two series expansions of the kernel
\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,x)
we get an interesting connection between the axially harmonic polynomials
\mathcal{H}_{k}^{n}(x)
and the axially polyharmonic polynomials
H_{k}(x)
.

Corollary 4.32
.

Let
n
be an odd number, set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
and assume that
1\leq\ell\leq h_{n}
.
Then, for
s
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
such that
|x|<|s|
we have

\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}H_{k}(x)s^{-2-k}\right)^{\ell}=\frac{1}{(2h_{n}-1)!}\sum_{k=2\ell-1}^{\infty}\sum_{\alpha=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\sum_{\beta=0}^{\alpha}K_{k,\alpha,\beta,\ell,h_{n}}\mathcal{H}^{n}_{k-2\alpha+\beta-2\ell+1}(x)(-2x_{0})^{\beta}|x|^{2(\alpha-\beta)}s^{-1-k},

where the constants
K_{k,\alpha,\beta,\ell,h_{n}}
are defined in (
4.38
)

Proof.

The result follows by setting the second hand side in (
4.37
) equal to the second hand side in (
4.52
).
∎

Finally we have all the tools to provide an integral representation for axially polyharmonic functions.

Theorem 4.33

(Integral representation for polyharmonic functions)
.

Let
n
be an odd number, set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
and
s
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be such that
s\notin[x]
. We assume that
U
is a bounded slice Cauchy domain in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. We set
ds_{I}=ds(-I)
with
I\in\mathbb{S}
and we recall that
\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,x)
is defined in (
4.30
).

1)

If
f
is a left slice hyperholomorphic function on a set that contains
\overline{U}
, then
\tilde{f}(x)=D\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}f(x)
, with
1\leq\ell\leq h_{n}
, is axially polyharmonic of degree
h_{n}-\ell+1
and it admits the integral representation

(4.53)

\tilde{f}(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,x)ds_{I}f(s),\qquad\hbox{for any}\quad x\in U.

2)

If
f
is a right slice hyperholomorphic function on a set that contains
\overline{U}
, then
\tilde{f}(x)=f(x)D\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}
, with
1\leq\ell\leq h_{n}
, is axially polyharmonic of degree
h_{n}-\ell+1
and it admits the integral representation

(4.54)

\tilde{f}(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}f(s)ds_{I}\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,x),\qquad\hbox{for any}\quad x\in U.

Moreover, the integrals in (
4.53
) and (
4.54
) depend neither on
U
and nor on the imaginary unit
I\in\mathbb{S}
and are independent on the kernel of
D\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}
.

Proof.

We prove only formula (
4.53
), since formula (
4.54
) follows by similar arguments. By Theorem
4.16
and Theorem
2.14
we have

\tilde{f}(x)=D\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}f(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}D\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}S^{-1}_{L}(s,x)ds_{I}f(s)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,x)ds_{I}f(s).

The independence of the integrals (
4.53
) and (
4.54
) from the set
U
and the imaginary unit follows from Theorem
2.14
. Finally, the regularity follows by Proposition
4.19
. Now, we show that formula (
4.53
) is independent from the kernel of
D\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}
. Let
f_{1}
be a function defined as
f
such that
\tilde{f}(x)=D\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}f_{1}(x)
. Thus by Lemma
4.29
we have

f_{1}(x)=f(x)+\sum_{\nu=0}^{2\ell-2}x^{\nu}\alpha_{\nu},\qquad\{\alpha_{\nu}\}_{0\leq\nu\leq 2\ell-2}.

Thus by (
4.53
) we have

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{1}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,x)ds_{I}f_{1}(s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,x)ds_{I}f(s)+\sum_{i=0}^{2\ell-2}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,x)ds_{I}s^{i}a_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{f}(x)+\sum_{i=0}^{2\ell-2}D\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}s^{i}a_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{f}(x)

∎

5.
A Polyharmonic functional calculus based on the
S
-spectrum

In this section our goal is to introduce a polyharmonic functional calculus by means of the integral representation of polyharmonic function studied in Section
4
.
The following diagram allows to visualize the polyharmonic fine structure, i.e., the function spaces and the related functional calculi:

\begin{CD}\mathcal{SH}_{L}(U)@>{{D}\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}}>{}>\mathcal{APH}^{L}_{h_{n}-\ell+1}(U)@>{\overline{D}\Delta_{n+1}^{h_{n}-\ell}}>{}>\mathcal{AM}_{L}(U)\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
S\text{-Functional calculus}\text{Polyharmonic Functional calculus }F\text{-Functional calculus}\\
\end{CD}

A similar diagram holds for functions in
\mathcal{SH}_{R}(U)
.
In this section we develop the functional calculus of the central part of the diagram.
From the definition of the axially harmonic polynomials, given by (
4.1
), we now provide the notion of Clifford-harmonic operators.

Definition 5.1

(The Clifford harmonic operators)
.

Let
n
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
T\in\mathcal{BC}^{0,1}(V_{n})
. We define the Clifford-harmonic operators as

(5.1)

\mathcal{H}_{k}^{n}(T)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\mathcal{T}_{\ell}^{k}(n)T^{k-\ell}\overline{T}^{\ell},\qquad\mathcal{T}_{\ell}^{k}(n)=\binom{k}{\ell}\frac{\left(\frac{n-1}{2}\right)_{k-\ell}\left(\frac{n-1}{2}\right)_{\ell}}{(n-1)_{k}}.

Now, we show a relation between the Clifford Appell operators (see (
3.19
)) and the Clifford harmonic-operators.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
. For
T\in\mathcal{BC}^{0,1}(V_{n})
and
\ell\geq 0
we have

(5.2)

\binom{\ell+1+2h_{n}}{\ell+1}P^{n}_{\ell+1}(T)-\binom{\ell+2h_{n}}{\ell}TP_{\ell}^{n}(T)=\binom{\ell+2h_{n}}{2h_{n}-1}\mathcal{H}^{n}_{\ell+1}(T).

Proof.

By the definition of the Clifford-Appell operators, see (
3.19
), we have

\displaystyle\binom{\ell+1+2h_{n}}{\ell+1}P^{n}_{\ell+1}(T)-\binom{\ell+2h_{n}}{\ell}TP_{\ell}^{n}(T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\binom{\ell+1+2h_{n}}{\ell+1}\sum_{\nu=0}^{\ell+1}\mathcal{C}_{\nu}^{\ell+1}(n)T^{\ell+1-\nu}\overline{T}^{\nu}-\binom{\ell+2h_{n}}{\ell}\sum_{\nu=0}^{\ell}\mathcal{C}_{\nu}^{\ell}(n)T^{\ell+1-\nu}\overline{T}^{\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\binom{\ell+1+2h_{n}}{\ell+1}\mathcal{C}_{\ell+1}^{\ell+1}(n)\overline{T}^{\ell+1}+\binom{\ell+1+2h_{n}}{\ell+1}\sum_{\nu=0}^{\ell}\mathcal{C}_{\nu}^{\ell+1}(n)T^{\ell+1-\nu}\overline{T}^{\nu}

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\binom{\ell+2h_{n}}{\ell}\sum_{\nu=0}^{\ell}\mathcal{C}_{\nu}^{\ell}(n)T^{\ell+1-\nu}\overline{T}^{\nu}.

By Lemma
4.21
, with
m=\ell+2h_{n}
, we get

\displaystyle\binom{\ell+1+2h_{n}}{\ell+1}P^{n}_{\ell+1}(T)-\binom{\ell+2h_{n}}{\ell}TP_{\ell}^{n}(T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\binom{\ell+2h_{n}}{2h_{n}-1}\mathcal{T}_{\ell+1}^{\ell+1}(n)\overline{T}^{\ell+1}+\binom{\ell+2h_{n}}{2h_{n}-1}\sum_{\nu=0}^{\ell}\mathcal{T}_{\nu}^{\ell+1}(n)T^{\ell+1-\nu}\overline{T}^{\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\binom{\ell+2h_{n}}{2h_{n}-1}\mathcal{H}^{n}_{\ell+1}(T).

∎

Definition 5.3

(Polyharmonic resolvent operator)
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
. We assume
T\in\mathcal{BC}^{0,1}(V_{n})
and
1\leq\ell\leq h_{n}
. Then, for
s\in\rho_{S}(T)
, we define the polyharmonic resolvent operator by

\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,T)=\sigma_{n,\ell}(s^{2}-2s(T+\overline{T})+T\overline{T})^{-\ell},

where
\sigma_{n,\ell}
is defined in (
4.31
).

Now we give a suitable series expansion for the polyharmonic resolvent operator

\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,T)
.

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
n
be an odd number and
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
, with
1\leq\ell\leq h_{n}
.
Assume that
T\in\mathcal{BC}^{0,1}(V_{n})
and
s\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
is such that
\|T\|<|s|
. Then, we have

\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,T)=\sigma_{n,\ell}\sum_{m=2h_{n}-1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\sum_{t=0}^{h_{n}-\ell-k}\mathbf{k}_{m,h_{n},t,\ell,k}|T|^{2t}\mathcal{H}^{n}_{m-2h_{n}+1}(T)(-2T_{0})^{h_{n}-\ell-k-t}s^{-1+h_{n}-\ell-k-t-m},

where

\mathbf{k}_{m,h_{n},t,\ell,k}:=\binom{m}{2h_{n}-1}\binom{h_{n}-\ell-k}{t}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{k},

the constants
\sigma_{n,\ell}
are given in (
4.31
).

Proof.

From the definition of the polyharmonic resolvent operator we have

(5.3)

\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,T)=\sigma_{n,\ell}\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(T)^{-\ell}=\sigma_{n,\ell}\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}^{-h_{n}}(T)\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}^{h_{n}-\ell}(T).

Thus to get the series expansion of
\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,T)
is crucial to obtain an expansion in series of
\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}^{-h_{n}}(T)
. By
[
38
, Thm. 5.1]
, Proposition
3.24
and Remark
3.21
we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}^{-h_{n}}(T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\gamma_{n}}\left[F_{n}^{L}(s,T)s-TF_{n}^{L}(s,T)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\sum_{m=2h_{n}}^{\infty}\binom{m}{m-2h_{n}}P^{n}_{m-2h_{n}}(T)s^{-m}-\sum_{m=2h_{n}}^{\infty}T\binom{m}{m-2h_{n}}P^{n}_{m-2h_{n}}(T)s^{-1-m}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m=2h_{n}-1}^{\infty}\left[\binom{m+1}{m+1-2h_{n}}P^{n}_{m+1-2h_{n}}(T)-T\binom{m}{m-2h_{n}}P^{n}_{m-2h_{n}}(T)\right]s^{-1-m}.

By using (
5.2
) with
\ell=m-2h_{n}
we obtain

(5.4)

\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}^{-h_{n}}(T)=\sum_{m=2h_{n}-1}^{\infty}\binom{m}{2h_{n}-1}\mathcal{H}^{n}_{m-2h_{n}+1}(T)s^{-1-m}.

By plugging (
5.4
) into (
5.3
) and by using the binomial theorem we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma_{n,\ell}\sum_{m=2h_{n}-1}^{\infty}\binom{m}{2h_{n}-1}\mathcal{H}^{n}_{m-2h_{n}+1}(T)s^{-1-m}\sum_{k=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{k}s^{2k}(|T|^{2}-2T_{0}s)^{h_{n}-\ell-k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma_{n,\ell}\sum_{m=2h_{n}-1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\sum_{t=0}^{h_{n}-\ell-k}\binom{m}{2h_{n}-1}\binom{h_{n}-\ell-k}{t}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{k}\times

\displaystyle\times|T|^{2t}\mathcal{H}^{n}_{m-2h_{n}+1}(T)(-2T_{0})^{h_{n}-\ell-k-t}s^{-1+h_{n}-\ell-k-t-m}.

∎

We can also write the polyharmonic resolvent operator in terms of the
F
-resolvent operator.

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
. For
s\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
,
T\in\mathcal{BC}^{0,1}(V_{n})
with
1\leq\ell\leq h_{n}
we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma_{n,\ell}}{\gamma_{n}}\sum_{k=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\sum_{t=0}^{h_{n}-\ell-k}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{k}\binom{h_{n}-\ell-k}{t}|T|^{2t}(-2T_{0})^{h_{n}-\ell-k-t}F_{n}^{L}(s,T)s^{h_{n}+k-\ell-t+1}

\displaystyle-\frac{\sigma_{n,\ell}}{\gamma_{n}}\sum_{k=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\sum_{t=0}^{h_{n}-\ell-k}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{k}\binom{h_{n}-\ell-k}{t}T|T|^{2t}(-2T_{0})^{h_{n}-\ell-k-t}F_{n}^{L}(s,T)s^{h_{n}+k-\ell-t}.

Proof.

By
[
38
, Thm. 5.1]
and the binomial Newton we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,T)=\sigma_{n,\ell}\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}^{-h_{n}}(T)\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}^{h_{n}-\ell}(T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma_{n,\ell}}{\gamma_{n}}\left(F_{n}^{L}(s,T)s-TF_{n}^{L}(s,T)\right)\sum_{k=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{k}s^{2k}\sum_{t=0}^{h_{n}-\ell-k}\binom{h_{n}-\ell-k}{t}|T|^{2t}(-2T_{0}s)^{h_{n}-\ell-k-t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma_{n,\ell}}{\gamma_{n}}\sum_{k=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\sum_{t=0}^{h_{n}-\ell-k}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{k}\binom{h_{n}-\ell-k}{t}|T|^{2t}(-2T_{0})^{h_{n}-\ell-k-t}F_{n}^{L}(s,T)s^{h_{n}+k-\ell-t+1}

\displaystyle-\frac{\sigma_{n,\ell}}{\gamma_{n}}\sum_{k=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\sum_{t=0}^{h_{n}-\ell-k}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{k}\binom{h_{n}-\ell-k}{t}T|T|^{2t}(-2T_{0})^{h_{n}-\ell-k-t}F_{n}^{L}(s,T)s^{h_{n}+k-\ell-t}.

∎

Remark 5.6
.

Observe that it is possible to show the result in Proposition
5.5
by means of the right
F
-resolvent operator
F_{n}^{R}(s,T)
by using the relation

\gamma_{n}\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}^{-h_{n}}(T)=sF_{n}^{R}(s,T)-F_{n}^{R}(s,T)T,

see
[
38
, Thm. 5.1]
.

For the definition of the functional calculus we have to
show the slice hyperholomorphicity in the variable
s
of the polyharmonic resolvent operator

\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,T)
, with
1\leq\ell\leq h_{n}
.
This fact is stated in the next lemma.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
n
be an odd number, set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
and
T\in\mathcal{BC}^{0,1}(V_{n})
. The polyharmonic resolvent operator
\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,T)
, with
1\leq\ell\leq h_{n}
, is a
\mathcal{B}(V_{n})
-valued right and left slice hyperholomorphic function of the variable
s
in
\rho_{S}(T)
.

Proof.

Since the operator
F_{n}^{L}(s,T)
is a
\mathcal{B}(V_{n})
-valued right slice hyperholomorphic in the variable
s
, see
[
38
]
. By Proposition
5.5
we get that the polyharmonic resolvent operator
\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,T)
is
\mathcal{B}(V_{n})
-valued right slice hyperholomorphic in the variable
s
. By Remark
5.6
we know that we can write the polyharmonic resolvent operator
\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,T)
in terms of
F_{n}^{R}(s,T)
. Since the right
F
-resolvent operator is
\mathcal{B}(V_{n})
-valued left slice hyperholomorphic in the variable
s
, see
[
38
]
, we get that
\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,T)
is also
\mathcal{B}(V_{n})
-valued left slice hyperholomorphic in the variable
s
.
∎

Definition 5.8

(Polyharmonic functional calculus on the
S
-spectrum)
.

Let
n
be an odd number, set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
and assume that
0\leq\ell\leq h_{n}
. Let
T\in\mathcal{BC}^{1}(V_{n})
be such that its components
T_{i}
,
i=1,...,n
have real spectra. Let
U
be a bounded slice Cauchy domain as in Definition
2.22
and set
ds_{I}=ds(-I)
for
I\in\mathbb{S}
.

•

For any
f\in\mathcal{SH}^{L}_{\sigma_{S}(T)}(U)
we set
\tilde{f}(x)=\overline{D}\Delta_{n+1}^{\ell-1}f(x)
. We define the polyharmonic functional calculus for the operator
T
as

(5.5)

\tilde{f}(T):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,T)ds_{I}f(s),

•

For any
f\in\mathcal{SH}^{R}_{\sigma_{S}(T)}(U)
we set
\tilde{f}(x)=\overline{D}\Delta_{n+1}^{\ell-1}f(x)
. We define the polyharmonic functional calculus for the operator
T
as

(5.6)

\tilde{f}(T):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}f(s)ds_{I}\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,T).

Remark 5.9
.

When we set
n
equal to
3
in the definition above, we get the harmonic functional calculus based on the
S
-spectrum, as it was shown in
[
32
]
.

Theorem 5.10
.

Let
n
be an odd number.
The polyharmonic functional calculus based on the
S
-spectrum is well defined, i.e. the integrals (
5.5
) and (
5.6
) depend neither on the imaginary units
J\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
nor on the slice Cauchy domain
U
.

Proof.

We show the result only for left slice hyperholomorphic functions, since the case for right slice hyperholomorphic function can be deduced by similar arguments.
We first show that the integral (
5.5
) does not depend on the slice domain
U
. Let
U^{\prime}
be another bounded slice Cauchy domain with
\sigma_{S}(T)\subset U^{\prime}
and
\overline{U^{\prime}}\subset\operatorname{dom}(f)
, and let us assume for the moment
\overline{U^{\prime}}\subset U
. Then
O=U\setminus U^{\prime}
is again a bounded slice Cauchy domain and we have
\overline{O}\subset\rho_{S}(T)
and
\overline{O}\subset\operatorname{dom}(f)
. Hence the function
f(s)
is left slice hyperholomorphic and the kernel
\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,T)
is right slice hyperholomorphic in the variable
s
on
\overline{O}
.
The Cauchy integral theorem therefore implies

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(O\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,T)\,ds_{I}\,f(s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,T)\,ds_{I}\,f(s)-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U^{\prime}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,T)\,ds_{I}\,f(s).

If
\overline{U^{\prime}}\not\subset U
, then
O:=U^{\prime}\cap U
is an axially symmetric that contains
\sigma_{S}(T)
. As in Remark 3.2.4 in
[
37
]
, we can hence find a third slice Cauchy domain
U^{\prime\prime}
with
\sigma_{S}(T)\subset U^{\prime\prime}
and
\overline{U^{\prime\prime}}\subset O=U\cap U^{\prime}
. The above arguments show that the integrals over the boundaries of all three sets agree.

In order to show the independence of the imaginary units, we choose two units
I,J\in\mathbb{S}
and the two slice Cauchy domains
U_{x},U_{s}\subset\operatorname{dom}(f)
with
\sigma_{S}(T)\subset U_{x}
and
\overline{U_{x}}\subset U_{s}
(the subscripts
s
and
x
are chosen in order to indicate the variable of integration in the following computation). The set
U^{c}_{x}=\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus U_{x}
is then an unbounded axially symmetric slice Cauchy domain with
\overline{U^{c}_{x}}\subset\rho_{S}(T)
. The left
\mathcal{K}
-resolvent is right slice hyperholomorphic on
\rho_{S}(T)
, see Lemma
5.7
, and also at infinity because

\lim_{s\to\infty}\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,T)=0.

The right slice hyperholomorphic Cauchy formula implies therefore

\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,T)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U^{c}_{x}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathcal{\mathbf{}}{H}_{\ell}(x,T)\,dx_{I}S^{-1}_{R}(x,s)

for every
s\in U^{c}_{x}
. Since by Corollary 2.1.26 in
[
37
]
we have
\partial(U^{c}_{x}\cap\mathbb{C}_{J})=-\partial(U_{x}\cap\mathbb{C}_{J})
and
S^{-1}_{R}(x,s)=-S^{-1}_{L}(s,x)
, we therefore obtain

\begin{split}\tilde{f}(T)&=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U_{s}\cap\mathbb{C}_{J})}\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,T)\,ds_{J}\,f(s)\\
&=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{\partial(U_{s}\cap\mathbb{C}_{J})}\left(\int_{\partial(U^{c}_{x}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(x,T)dx_{I}S^{-1}_{R}(x,s)\right)\,ds_{J}f(s)\\
&=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{\partial(U_{x}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(x,T)dx_{I}\left(\int_{\partial(U_{s}\cap\mathbb{C}_{J})}S^{-1}_{L}(s,x)\,ds_{J}f(s)\right)\\
&=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U_{x}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(x,T)\,dx_{I}\,f(x),\end{split}

where the last identity follows again from the slice hyperholomorphic Cauchy formula because we chose
\overline{U_{x}}\subset U_{s}
.
∎

Proposition 5.11
.

Let
n
be an odd integer, and define
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
with
1\leq\ell\leq h_{n}
. Suppose
T\in\mathcal{BC}^{0,1}(V_{n})
and
f\in\mathcal{N}_{\sigma_{S}(T)}(U)
, where
U
is a bounded slice Cauchy domain as specified in Definition
2.22
.
Then we have

\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,T)\,ds_{I}\,f(s)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}f(s)ds_{I}\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,T).

Proof.

The result follows easily from the by Lemma
5.7
and the fact that we are considering an intrinsic slice hyperholomorphic function.
∎

The following technical result based on the monogenic functional calculus will be important in sequel.

Theorem 5.12
.

Let
n
be an odd number,
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
and
1\leq\ell\leq h_{n}
. Let
T\in\mathcal{BC}^{1}(V_{n})
and assume that the operators
T_{i}
, for
i=1,2,...,n
, have real spectrum. Let
G
be a bounded slice Cauchy domain such that
(\partial G)\cap\sigma_{S}(T)=\emptyset
. Thus, for every
I\in\mathbb{S}
we have

\int_{\partial(G\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,T)ds_{I}s^{\alpha}=0,\qquad\hbox{if}\quad 0\leq\alpha\leq 2(\ell-1).

Proof.

By the Fueter-Sce theorem, see Theorem
3.19
, we have

(5.7)

\int_{\partial(G\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}F_{n}^{L}(s,x)ds_{I}s^{m}=\Delta^{h_{n}}_{n+1}(x^{m})=0,\qquad\hbox{if}\quad m\leq 2h_{n}-1,

for all
x\notin\partial G
and
I\in\mathbb{S}
. Now, by using Proposition
5.5
, Fubini theorem and Proposition
3.42
we have

(5.8)

\displaystyle\int_{\partial(G\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,T)ds_{I}s^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma_{n,\ell}}{\gamma_{n}}\sum_{k=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\sum_{t=0}^{h_{n}-\ell-k}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{k}\binom{h_{n}-\ell-k}{t}|T|^{2t}(-2T_{0})^{h_{n}-\ell-k-t}

\displaystyle\times\int_{\partial(G\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}F_{n}^{L}(s,T)ds_{I}s^{h_{n}+k-\ell-t+1+\alpha}

\displaystyle-\frac{\sigma_{n,\ell}}{\gamma_{n}}\sum_{k=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\sum_{t=0}^{h_{n}-\ell-k}\binom{h_{n}-\ell-k}{t}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{k}T|T|^{2t}(-2T_{0})^{h_{n}-\ell-k-t}

\displaystyle\times\int_{\partial(G\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}F_{n}^{L}(s,T)ds_{I}s^{h_{n}+k-\ell-t+\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma_{n,\ell}}{\gamma_{n}}\sum_{k=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\sum_{t=0}^{h_{n}-\ell-k}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{k}\binom{h_{n}-\ell-k}{t}|T|^{2t}(-2T_{0})^{h_{n}-\ell-k-t}

\displaystyle\times\int_{\partial W}G(\omega,T)D\omega\left(\int_{\partial(G\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}F_{n}^{L}(s,\omega)ds_{I}s^{h_{n}+k-\ell-t+1+\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{\sigma_{n,\ell}}{\gamma_{n}}\sum_{k=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\sum_{t=0}^{h_{n}-\ell-k}\binom{h_{n}-\ell-k}{t}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{k}T|T|^{2t}(-2T_{0})^{h_{n}-\ell-k-t}

\displaystyle\times\int_{\partial W}G(\omega,T)D\omega\left(\int_{\partial(G\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}F_{n}^{L}(s,\omega)ds_{I}s^{h_{n}+k-\ell-t+\alpha}\right),

where
W\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
as in Definition
3.33
.
Since
k\leq h_{n}-\ell
,
\alpha\leq 2(\ell-1)
and
t\geq 0
we have

h_{n}+k-\ell-t+1+\alpha\leq 2h_{n}-2\ell-t+1+2\ell-2=2h_{n}-t-1\leq 2h_{n}-1.

Thus by (
5.7
) we have

(5.9)

\int_{\partial(G\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}F_{n}^{L}(s,\omega)ds_{I}s^{h_{n}+k-\ell-t+1+\alpha}=0.

Similarly, we have that
h_{n}+k-\ell-t+\alpha\leq 2h_{n}-1
and so by (
5.7
) we have

(5.10)

\int_{\partial(G\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}F_{n}^{L}(s,\omega)ds_{I}s^{h_{n}+k-\ell-t+\alpha}=0.

Finally, the result follows by plugging into (
5.8
) the expressions (
5.9
) and (
5.10
).
∎

Proposition 5.13
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
. We assume
T\in\mathcal{BC}^{1}(V_{n})
be such
T_{i}
,
i=1,...,n
have real spectra. Let
U
be slice Cauchy domain with
\sigma_{S}(T)\subset U
. Let
f
,
g\in\mathcal{SH}^{L}_{\sigma_{S}(T)}(U)
(resp.
f
,
g\in\mathcal{SH}^{R}_{\sigma_{S}(T)}(U)
) and such that

D\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}f(x)=D\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}g(x)
( resp.
f(x)D\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}=g(x)D\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1})
with
1\leq\ell\leq h_{n}
, then we have
\tilde{f}(T)=\tilde{g}(T)
.

Proof.

We show the result only for the left slice hyperholomorphic functions, the case for right slice hyperholomorphic functions follows by using similar arguments. We assume that
U
is connected. By the definition of the polyharmonic functional calculus, see Definition
5.8
, we have

\tilde{f}(T)-\tilde{g}(T)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,T)ds_{I}(f(s)-g(s)).

By Lemma
5.7
we know that the resolvent operator
\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,T)
is slice hyperholomorphic in
s
, thus we can change the domain of integration to
B_{r}(0)\cap\mathbb{C}_{I}
for
r>0
such that
\|T\|<r
. We know by hypothesis that
f-g
belongs to the kernel of the operator
D\Delta^{\ell-1}_{n+1}
, thus by Lemma
4.29
we get

\tilde{f}(T)-\tilde{g}(T)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{\nu=0}^{2\ell-2}\int_{\partial(B_{r}(0)\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,T)ds_{I}s^{\nu}\alpha_{\nu},

where
\{\alpha_{\nu}\}_{0\leq\nu\leq 2\ell-2}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{n}
. By Theorem
5.12
(since
\nu\leq 2(\ell-1)
) we know that the previous integral is zero.

In the case the set
U
is not connected we can write
U=\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{n}U_{\ell}
where
U_{\ell}
are the connected components of the set
U
. So by Lemma
4.29
we have

f(s)-g(s)=\sum_{\tau=1}^{n}\sum_{\nu=0}^{2\ell-2}\chi_{U_{\tau}}s^{\nu}\alpha_{\nu,\tau}.

Hence by the definition of the polyharmonic functional calculus, see Definition
5.8
, we have

\tilde{f}(T)-\tilde{g}(T)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{\tau=1}^{n}\sum_{\nu=0}^{2\ell-2}\int_{\partial(U_{\tau}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,T)ds_{I}s^{\nu}\alpha_{\nu,\tau}.

Finally, by Theorem
5.12
we get that
\tilde{f}(T)=\tilde{g}(T)
.
∎

Now, we show some algebraic properties of the polyharmonic functional calculus.

Lemma 5.14
.

Let
n
be an odd number, set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
and let
1\leq\ell\leq h_{n}
. We assume
T\in\mathcal{BC}^{1}(V_{n})
be such that
T_{i}
, for
i=1,...,n
,
have real spectra. Then, if
\tilde{f}(x)=D\Delta_{n+1}^{\ell-1}f(x)
(resp.
\tilde{f}(x)=f(x)D\Delta_{n+1}^{\ell-1}
) and
\tilde{g}(x)=D\Delta_{n+1}^{\ell-1}g(x)
(resp.
\tilde{g}(x)=g(x)D\Delta_{n+1}^{\ell-1}
) with
f
,
g\in\mathcal{SH}^{L}_{\sigma_{S}(T)}(U)
(resp.
\mathcal{SH}^{R}_{\sigma_{S}(T)}(U)
) and
a\in\mathbb{R}_{n}
,
then

(\tilde{f}a+\tilde{g})(T)=\tilde{f}(T)a+\tilde{g}(T),\qquad(resp.\ \ (a\tilde{f}+\tilde{g})(T)=a\tilde{f}(T)+\tilde{g}(T)).

Proof.

The result follows from the linearity of the integral (
5.5
) (resp. (
5.6
)).
∎

Proposition 5.15
.

Let
n
be an odd number, set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
and let
1\leq\ell\leq h_{n}
. We assume
T\in\mathcal{BC}^{1}(V_{n})
such that
T_{i}
,
i=1,...,n
have real spectra.
Recall that the constants
\sigma_{n,\ell}
and
\gamma_{n}
are defined in (
4.31
) and
(
3.12
), respectively.

•

Let
\tilde{f}(x)=D\Delta_{n+1}^{\ell-1}f(x)
where
f(x)=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}x^{m}a_{m}
with
a_{m}\in\mathbb{R}_{n}
, converges on a ball
B_{r}(0)
, for
r>0
, with
\sigma_{S}(T)\subset B_{r}(0)
. Then, we have

\tilde{f}(T)=\sigma_{n,\ell}\sum_{m=2h_{n}-1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\sum_{t=0}^{h_{n}-\ell-k}\mathbf{k}_{m,h_{n},t,\ell,k}|T|^{2t}\mathcal{H}^{n}_{m-2h_{n}+1}(T)(-2T_{0})^{h_{n}-\ell-k-t}a_{\ell-k+t+m-h_{n}}.

•

Let
\tilde{f}(x)=f(x)D\Delta_{n+1}^{\ell-1}
where
f(x)=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}a_{m}x^{m}
with
a_{m}\in\mathbb{R}_{n}
, converges on a ball
B_{r}(0)
, for
r>0
, with
\sigma_{S}(T)\subset B_{r}(0)
.
Then, we have

\tilde{f}(T)=\sigma_{n,\ell}\sum_{m=2h_{n}-1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\sum_{t=0}^{h_{n}-\ell-k}a_{\ell-k+t+m-h_{n}}\mathbf{k}_{m,h_{n},t,\ell,k}|T|^{2t}\mathcal{H}^{n}_{m-2h_{n}+1}(T)(-2T_{0})^{h_{n}-\ell-k-t}.

Proof.

We prove only the first statement since the second one follows from similar arguments. We pick an imaginary unit

I\in\mathbb{S}
and a radius
R
, with
0<R<r
, such that
\sigma_{S}(T)\subset B_{R}(0)
. Thus, by Definition
5.8
and the fact that the series expansion of
f
converges uniformly on
\partial(B_{R}(0)\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})
, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{f}(T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(B_{R}(0)\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,T)ds_{I}\sum_{\tau=0}^{\infty}s^{\tau}a_{\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{\tau=0}^{\infty}\int_{\partial(B_{R}(0)\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,T)ds_{I}s^{\tau}a_{\tau}.

By using the series expansion of the resolvent operator
\mathbf{H}_{\ell}(s,T)
obtained in Proposition
5.4
we have

\displaystyle\tilde{f}(T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma_{n,\ell}}{2\pi}\sum_{\tau=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=2h_{n}-1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\sum_{t=0}^{h_{n}-\ell-k}\mathbf{k}_{m,h_{n},t,\ell,k}|T|^{2t}\mathcal{H}^{n}_{m-2h_{n}+1}(T)(-2T_{0})^{h_{n}-\ell-k-t}\times

\displaystyle\times\int_{\partial(B_{R}(0)\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}s^{-1+h_{n}-\ell+k-t-m+\tau}a_{\tau}ds_{I}.

Now, by using the fact that

\int_{\partial(B_{R}(0)\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}s^{-1-\alpha+\tau}ds_{I}=\begin{cases}2\pi,\qquad\hbox{if}\quad\tau=\alpha\\
0,\qquad\hbox{if}\quad\tau\neq\alpha,\end{cases}

and by setting
\alpha:=-h_{n}+\ell-k+t+m
we get

\tilde{f}(T)=\sigma_{n,\ell}\sum_{m=2h_{n}-1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\sum_{t=0}^{h_{n}-\ell-k}\mathbf{k}_{m,h_{n},t,\ell,k}|T|^{2t}\mathcal{H}^{n}_{m-2h_{n}+1}(T)(-2T_{0})^{h_{n}-\ell-k-t}a_{\ell-k+t+m-h_{n}}.

This proves the result.
∎

6.
Holomorphic Cliffordian functions in integral form

The notion of the holomorphic Cliffordian function was first introduced by G. Laville and I. Ramadanoff in
[
77
]
. In that paper, the authors study integral representations and the counterparts of the Taylor and Laurent expansions for this type of functions. In subsequent papers, see
[
73
,
74
,
75
,
76
]
, the same authors further develop the concept of the holomorphic Cliffordian functions.

Definition 6.1

(Axially holomorphic Cliffordian)
.

Let
U
be an open set in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
and
k\geq 0
. A function
f:\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\to\mathbb{R}_{n}
of class
\mathcal{C}^{2k+1}(U)
is said to be left (resp. right) axially holomorphic Cliffordian if it is of axial type, see (
3.1
), and if we have

\Delta^{k}_{n+1}Df(x)=0,\qquad\forall x\in U,\qquad\left(\hbox{reps.}\quad f(x)\Delta^{k}_{n+1}D=0,\qquad\forall x\in U\right)

In
[
33
]
the authors pointed out that this class of functions arises naturally in the factorization of the Fueter-Sce map.
Moreover, by making a suitable factorization of
\Delta^{h_{n}}_{n+1}
we also have the class of functions given by:

Definition 6.2

(Axially polyanalytic holomorphic Cliffordian of order
(k,\ell)
)
.

Let
U
be an open set in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
and
k
,
\ell\geq 0
. A function
f:\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\to\mathbb{R}_{n}
of class
\mathcal{C}^{2k+\ell}(U)
is said to be left (resp. right)
axially polyanalytic holomorphic Cliffordian of order
(k,\ell)
if it is of axial type, see (
3.1
), and if we have

\Delta^{k}_{n+1}D^{\ell}f(x)=0,\qquad\forall x\in U,\qquad\left(f(x)\Delta^{k}_{n+1}D^{\ell}=0,\qquad\forall x\in U\right).

Remark 6.3
.

Our goal is to introduce a holomorphic Cliffordian functional calculus based on the factorization of the Fueter-Sce map. This will be of crucial importance in Subsection
7.1
also for the product rule for the
F
-functional calculus.

However, before we proceed, we need to determine the appropriate factorization
of the Fueter-Sce map in order to obtain functions as in Definition
6.2
.

In this section, we will demonstrate each result solely for left slice hyperholomorphic functions, as analogous results for right slice hyperholomorphic functions follow from similar reasoning.

Before to figure out the suitbale factorization of the Fueter-Sce map that leads to the axially polyanalytic holomorphic Cliffordian functions, we need the following result.

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. If we assume that
f
is a function of axial type then we have that
\overline{D}^{k}f
is also of axial type. Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
, then we have that
D^{\alpha}\overline{D}^{k}f
is of axial type as well.

Proof.

First we show that
\overline{D}^{k}f
is of axial type by induction on
k
. If
k=1
, by (
4.6
) we get that
\overline{D}^{k}f
is of axial type. We suppose that
\overline{D}^{k}f
is of axial type, i.e.

\overline{D}^{k}f(x)=A(u,v)+IB(u,v),\qquad x=u+Iv,

where
A(u,v)
and
B(u,v)
are Clifford-valued functions. We have to show that
\overline{D}^{k+1}f
is also of axial type. By (
4.6
) and the inductive hypothesis we have

\displaystyle\overline{D}^{k+1}f(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{D}\left(A(u,v)+IB(u,v)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{u}A(u,v)+\partial_{v}B(u,v)+\frac{2h_{n}}{v}B(u,v))\right)+I\left(\partial_{u}B(u,v)-\partial_{v}A(u,v)\right).

This shows that
\overline{D}^{k}f(x)
is of axial type. Now we prove that
D^{\alpha}\overline{D}^{k}f
is of axial type by induction on
\alpha
. If
\alpha=1
, by (
4.5
) we have that

D\overline{D}^{k}f(x)=\left(\partial_{u}A(u,v)-\partial_{v}B(u,v)-\frac{2h_{n}}{v}B(u,v))\right)+I\left(\partial_{u}B(u,v)+\partial_{v}A(u,v)\right).

Now, we suppose that
D^{\alpha}\overline{D}^{k}f
is of axial type:

D^{\alpha}\overline{D}^{k}f=C(u,v)+ID(u,v),

where
C(u,v)
and
D(u,v)
are Clifford-valued functions. Thus by (
4.5
) and the inductive hypothesis we get

\displaystyle D^{\alpha+1}\overline{D}^{k}f(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D\left(C(u,v)+ID(u,v)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{u}C(u,v)-\partial_{v}D(u,v)-\frac{2h_{n}}{v}D(u,v))\right)+I\left(\partial_{u}D(u,v)+\partial_{v}C(u,v)\right).

This shows that
D^{\alpha+1}\overline{D}^{k}f(x)
is of axial type, which completes the proof of the result.
∎

Theorem 6.5
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
. We consider the parameters
\ell
and
s
in the set of natural numbers such that
0\leq\ell\leq h_{n}-2
and
0\leq s\leq h_{n}-1
with
s+\ell<h_{n}
. Then, the action of the operator

(6.1)

T_{FS}^{(II)}:=D^{s}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell-1}

on the set of left (resp. right) slice hyperholomorphic functions ,defined on an axially symmetric open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
,
gives left (resp. right) holomorphic Cliffordian functions of order
(\ell+1,h_{n}-s-\ell)
in
U
.

Proof.

Let
f\in\mathcal{SH}_{L}(U)
. By Lemma
6.4
it is clear that
T_{FS}^{(II)}f(x)
is of axial type. Now, by Definition
6.2
we have to show that

\Delta^{\ell+1}_{n+1}D^{h_{n}-s-\ell}T_{FS}^{(II)}f(x)=0,\qquad\forall x\in U.

By the Fueter-Sce mapping theorem, see Theorem
3.13
, and the fact that
\Delta_{n+1}=D\overline{D}
we have

\displaystyle\Delta^{\ell+1}_{n+1}D^{h_{n}-s-\ell}T_{FS}^{(II)}f(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta^{\ell+1}_{n+1}D^{h_{n}-\ell}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell-1}f(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D^{\ell+1}\overline{D}^{\ell+1}D^{h-\ell}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell-1}f(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle DD^{h_{n}}\overline{D}^{h_{n}}f(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta_{n+1}^{h_{n}}Df(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

∎

Definition 6.6

(Axially holomorphic Cliffordian of order
(k,\ell)
associated with
\mathcal{SH}_{L}(U)
and
\mathcal{SH}_{R}(U)
)
.

Let
n
be an odd number and
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
. We consider the parameters
\ell
and
s
in the set of natural numbers such that
0\leq\ell\leq h_{n}-2
and
0\leq s\leq h_{n}-1
with
s+\ell<h_{n}
.
Let
U
be an axially symmetric open set in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
and let
k
,
\ell\geq 0
.
The set of functions

\mathcal{AHC}^{L}_{\ell+1,h_{n}-s-\ell}(U)=\{D^{s}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell-1}f\ :\ f\in\mathcal{SH}(U)\}

is called left axially holomorphic Cliffordian of order
(\ell+1,h_{n}-s-\ell)
associated with
\mathcal{SH}_{L}(U)
and the set

\mathcal{AHC}^{R}_{\ell+1,h_{n}-s-\ell}(U)=\{fD^{s}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell-1}\ :\ f\in\mathcal{SH}_{R}(U)\}

is called right axially holomorphic Cliffordian of order
(\ell+1,h_{n}-s-\ell)
associated with
\mathcal{SH}_{R}(U)
.

As consequence of Theorem
6.5
we have the following result.

Corollary 6.7
.

Let
U
be an open set and
n
be an odd number such that
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
. Then for
0\leq\ell\leq h_{n}-2
and
0\leq s\leq h_{n}-1
with
s+\ell<h_{n}
we have the following facatorization of the Fueter-Sce construction:

\begin{CD}&&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{SH}_{L}(U)}@>{\ \ D^{s}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell-1}}>{}>{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{ACH}^{L}_{\ell+1,h_{n}-s-\ell}(U)}@>{\ \ D^{h_{n}-s}\overline{D}^{\ell+1}}>{}>{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{AM}_{L}(U)}.\end{CD}

Remark 6.8
.

For our purposes we define a holomorphic Cliffordian functional calculus for some specific values of the
indexes
\ell
and
s
in the operator
T_{FS}^{(II)}:=D^{s}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell-1}
defined in
(
6.1
). Let us consider
1\leq\alpha\leq h_{n}-1
.
We take
s=\alpha
and
\ell=h_{n}-1-\alpha
. These conditions on the indexes
s
and
\ell
stated in Theorem
6.5

are satisfied and it is also clear that
s+\ell<h_{n}
.
Thus, in this case, we get a holomorphic Cliffordian function of order

(h_{n}-\alpha,1)
that leads to focus on a particular operator of
T_{FS}^{(II)}
, that is given by

D^{\alpha}\overline{D}^{\alpha}=\Delta_{n+1}^{\alpha},\qquad 1\leq\alpha\leq h_{n}-1.

The fine structure we study is associated with the function space of axially holomorphic Cliffordian functions of order
(h_{n}-\alpha,1)
. The reason why we are considering the above operator among those that we can pick in (
6.1
) will be clarified in Subsection
7.1
where we show the product rule for the
F
-functional calculus.

Remark 6.9
.

Let
n
be an odd number and
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
and assume the natural number
\alpha
be such that

1\leq\alpha<h_{n}-1
.
Let
U
be an axially symmetric open set in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. We will study the fine structure associated with
the set of functions

\mathcal{AHC}^{L}_{h_{n}-\alpha,1}(U)=\{\Delta_{n+1}^{\alpha}f\ :\ f\in\mathcal{SH}_{L}(U)\}.

that are called left axially holomorphic Cliffordian of order
(h_{n}-\alpha,1)
associated with
\mathcal{SH}_{L}(U)
.
Similarly

\mathcal{AHC}^{R}_{h_{n}-\alpha,1}(U)=\{\Delta_{n+1}^{\alpha}f\ :\ f\in\mathcal{SH}_{R}(U)\}.

is called right axially holomorphic Cliffordian of order
(h_{n}-\alpha,1)
associated with
\mathcal{SH}_{R}(U)
.

By Proposition
4.15
we know that the action of the operator
\Delta_{n+1}^{\alpha}
to the left (resp. right) slice hyperholomorphic kernel Cauchy is given by

(6.2)

\Delta_{n+1}^{\alpha}S^{-1}_{L}(s,x)=4^{\alpha}\alpha!(-h_{n})_{\alpha}(s-\overline{x})\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-\alpha-1},
