\left(\hbox{resp.}\quad\Delta_{n+1}^{\alpha}S^{-1}_{R}(s,x)=4^{\alpha}\alpha!(-h_{n})_{\alpha}\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-\alpha-1}(s-\overline{x})\right).

The above formulas suggest the definition of the following kernels.

Definition 6.10
.

Let
n
be an odd number, set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
and let
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
1\leq\alpha\leq h_{n}-1
.
Let
s
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
with
s\notin[x]
. We define the left
\mathcal{K}
-kernel as

\mathcal{K}^{L}_{\alpha}(s,x)=k_{\alpha}(s-\overline{x})\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-\alpha-1}

and the right
\mathcal{K}
-kernel as

\mathcal{K}^{R}_{\alpha}(s,x)=k_{\alpha}\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-\alpha-1}(s-\overline{x}),

where

(6.3)

k_{\alpha}=4^{\alpha}\alpha!(-h_{n})_{\alpha}.

As for the
F
-kernels and the polyharmonic kernel also the
\mathcal{K}
-kernels have
two different regularities with respect to the two variables
x
and
s
.

Proposition 6.11
.

Let
n
be an odd number, set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
and let
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
1\leq\alpha\leq h_{n}-1
.
Let
s
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
for
x\notin[s]
. Then, we have that
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}^{L}(s,x)
(resp.
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}^{R}(s,x)
) is right (resp. left) slice hyperholomorphic in the variable
s
and left (resp. right) holomorphic Cliffordian function of order
(h_{n}-\alpha,1)
in the variable
x
.

Proof.

By the definition of the left slice hyperholomorphic Cauchy kernel
S^{-1}_{L}(s,x)
, see Definition
2.11
, we can write the left
\mathcal{K}
-kernel as

\mathcal{K}^{L}_{\alpha}(s,x)=k_{\alpha}S^{-1}_{L}(s,x)\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-\alpha}.

Hence, by Proposition
4.19
and the fact that
S^{-1}_{L}(s,x)
is right slice hyperholomorphic in the variable
s
we get that also the left
\mathcal{K}
-kernel is right slice hyperholomorphic in the variable
s
. Now, we show that the kernel
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}^{L}(s,x)
if of axial type.
By easy manipulations we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{L}_{\alpha}(s,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k_{\alpha}(s-\overline{x})\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(x)^{-\alpha-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{k_{\alpha}}{\gamma_{n}}\gamma_{n}(s-\overline{x})\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}^{-h_{n}-1}(x)\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}^{h_{n}-\alpha}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{k_{\alpha}}{\gamma_{n}}F_{n}^{L}(s,x)\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}^{h_{n}-\alpha}(x).

By Proposition
3.16
we know that
F_{n}^{L}(s,x)
is of axial type and so by using the Newton binomial we can write

\mathcal{K}^{L}_{\alpha}(s,x)=A_{2}(x_{0},|\underline{x}|)+\underline{\omega}B_{2}(x_{0},|\underline{x}|),

where
A_{2}(x_{0},|\underline{x}|)
and
B_{2}(x_{0},|\underline{x}|)
are suitable functions. The regularity of
\mathcal{K}^{L}_{\alpha}(s,x)
on the variable
x
follows by Theorem
6.5
and formula (
6.2
).
∎

We provide a series expansion of the left (resp. right)
\mathcal{K}
- kernel.
This can be obtained by using the series expansion of the
F
-kernel, see Proposition
3.24
.

Proposition 6.12
.

Let
n
be an odd number, set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
and let
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
1\leq\alpha\leq h_{n}-1
. Then for
x
,
s\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
such that
|x|<|s|
we have

(6.4)

\mathcal{K}^{L}_{\alpha}(s,x)=k_{\alpha}\sum_{k=2\alpha}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha}\sum_{\nu=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}\kappa_{k,h_{n},\alpha,\ell,\nu}P^{n}_{k-2\alpha-2\ell+\nu}(x)(-2x_{0})^{\nu}|x|^{2(\ell-\nu)}s^{-1-k},

and

(6.5)

\mathcal{K}^{R}_{\alpha}(s,x)=k_{\alpha}\sum_{k=2\alpha}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha}\sum_{\nu=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}\kappa_{k,h_{n},\alpha,\ell,\nu}s^{-1-k}P^{n}_{k-2\alpha-2\ell+\nu}(x)(-2x_{0})^{\nu}|x|^{2(\ell-\nu)},

where

(6.6)

\kappa_{k,h_{n},\alpha,\ell,\nu}:=\binom{h_{n}-\alpha}{\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}{\nu}\binom{k+2h_{n}-2\alpha-2\ell+\nu}{k-2\alpha-2\ell+\nu},

k_{\alpha}
is given in (
6.3
) and
\gamma_{n}
is defined in (
3.12
).

Proof.

By Proposition
6.11
we deduce that

\mathcal{K}^{L}_{\alpha}(s,x)=\frac{k_{\alpha}}{\gamma_{n}}F_{n}^{L}(s,x)\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}^{h_{n}-\alpha}(x).

Now by Proposition
3.17
and the Newton binomial we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{L}_{\alpha}(s,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{k_{\alpha}}{\gamma_{n}}F_{n}^{L}(s,x)\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}^{h_{n}-\alpha}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{k_{\alpha}}{\gamma_{n}}\sum_{k=2h_{n}}^{\infty}\binom{k}{k-2h_{n}}P^{n}_{k-2h_{n}}(x)s^{-1-k}(s^{2}-2x_{0}s+|x|^{2})^{h_{n}-\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k_{\alpha}\sum_{k=2h_{n}}^{\infty}\binom{k}{k-2h_{n}}P^{n}_{k-2h_{n}}(x)s^{-1-k}\sum_{\ell=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha}\binom{h-\alpha}{\ell}s^{2h_{n}-2\alpha-2\ell}(|x|^{2}-2x_{0}s)^{\ell}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k_{\alpha}\sum_{k=2h_{n}}^{\infty}\binom{k}{k-2h_{n}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha}\binom{h_{n}-\alpha}{\ell}P^{n}_{k-2h_{n}}(x)s^{-1-k+2h_{n}-2\alpha-2\ell}\times

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\nu=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}{\nu}(-2x_{0}s)^{\nu}|x|^{2\ell-2\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k_{\alpha}\sum_{\ell=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha}\sum_{\nu=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}\sum_{k=2h_{n}}^{\infty}\binom{k}{k-2h_{n}}\binom{h_{n}-\alpha}{\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}{\nu}\times

\displaystyle\times P^{n}_{k-2h_{n}}(x)s^{-1-k+2h_{n}-2\alpha-2\ell+\nu}(-2x_{0})^{\nu}|x|^{2(\ell-\nu)}.

Now, we change index
k
in the above sum with
u=k-2h_{n}+2\alpha+2\ell-\nu
, and by (
3.16
) we get

(6.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{L}_{\alpha}(s,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k_{\alpha}\sum_{\ell=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha}\sum_{\nu=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}\sum_{u=2\alpha+2\ell-\nu}^{\infty}\binom{u+2h_{n}-2\alpha-2\ell+\nu}{u-2\alpha-2\ell+\nu}\binom{h_{n}-\alpha}{\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}{\nu}\times

\displaystyle\times P^{n}_{u-2\alpha-2\ell+\nu}(x)s^{-1-u}(-2x_{0})^{\nu}|x|^{2(\ell-\nu)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{k_{\alpha}}{\gamma_{n}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha}\sum_{\nu=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}\sum_{u=2\alpha+2\ell-\nu}^{\infty}\binom{h_{n}-\alpha}{\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}{\nu}\Delta_{n+1}^{h_{n}}(x^{u+2h_{n}-2\alpha-2\ell+\nu})\times

\displaystyle\times s^{-1-u}(-2x_{0})^{\nu}|x|^{2(\ell-\nu)}.

Now, if
2\alpha\leq u<2\alpha+2\ell-\nu
then
\Delta_{n+1}^{h_{n}}(x^{u+2h_{n}-2\alpha-2\ell+\nu})=0
. This follows from the fact that
u+2h_{n}-2\alpha-2\ell+\nu<2h_{n}
. Thus we can write the series in (
6.7
) as

(6.8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{L}_{\alpha}(s,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k_{\alpha}\sum_{\ell=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha}\sum_{\nu=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}\sum_{u=2\alpha}^{\infty}\binom{h_{n}-\alpha}{\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}{\nu}\Delta_{n+1}^{h_{n}}(x^{u+2h_{n}-2\alpha-2\ell+\nu})\times

\displaystyle\times(-2x_{0})^{\nu}|x|^{2(\ell-\nu)}s^{-1-u}.

We get the final result by applying (
3.16
).
∎

From the previous result we can get how the operator
\Delta^{\alpha}_{n+1}
acts on the monomial
x^{k}
.

Corollary 6.13
.

Let
n
be an odd number, set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
and let
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
1\leq\alpha\leq h_{n}-1
. Let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
,
then, for
k\geq 1
, we have

(6.9)

\Delta^{\alpha}_{n+1}x^{k}=k_{\alpha}\sum_{\ell=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha}\sum_{\nu=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}\kappa_{k,h_{n},\alpha,\ell,\nu}P^{n}_{k-2\alpha-2\ell+\nu}(x)(-2x_{0})^{\nu}|x|^{2(\ell-\nu)},

where
\kappa_{k,h_{n},\alpha,\ell,\nu}
is defined in (
6.6
),

k_{\alpha}
is given in (
6.3
) and
\gamma_{n}
is defined in (
3.12
).

Proof.

By (
6.2
) we get that
\Delta^{\alpha}_{n+1}S^{-1}_{L}(s,x)=\mathcal{K}_{L}(s,x)
. By Proposition
2.9
we have that

\mathcal{K}^{L}_{\alpha}(s,x)=\sum_{k=2\alpha}^{\infty}\Delta^{\alpha}_{n+1}x^{k}s^{-1-k},\qquad|x|<|s|.

By using the expansion in (
6.12
), and the fact that two power series coincide when they have the same coefficients, we get (
6.9
).
∎

Now, we focus on finding the sum of the coefficients in (
6.9
). This will be of crucial importance in Section 7. Before we proceed we need the following preliminary result.

Theorem 6.14
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
.
Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be an axially symmetric open set that intersects the real line and
let
f\in\mathcal{SH}_{L}(U)
.
Thus, we have

(6.10)

\lim_{v\to 0}\Delta^{m}_{n+1}f(x)=2^{2m}(-1)^{m}m!\frac{(h_{n}-m+1)_{m}}{(2m)!}\partial_{u}^{2m}(f(u)),

where
f(x)=\alpha(u,v)+I\beta(u,v)
, for any
x=u+Ix
such that
(u,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
f(u)=\alpha(u,0)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.7
we have

(6.11)

\lim_{v\to 0}\Delta^{m}_{n+1}f(x)=2^{m}(h_{n}-m+1)_{m}\lim_{v\to 0}\left[\left(\frac{1}{v}\partial_{v}\right)^{m}\alpha(u,v)+I\left(\partial_{v}\frac{1}{v}\right)^{m}\beta(u,v)\right].

By Lemma
4.5
and Lemma
4.8
we have

\lim_{v\to 0}\left(\frac{1}{v}\partial_{v}\right)^{m}\alpha(u,v)=\lim_{v\to 0}\sum_{j=m}^{\infty}\frac{2^{m}(-1)^{j}j!v^{2j-2m}}{(2j)!(j-m)!}\partial_{u}^{2j}(f(u)).

In the previous summation the only term that is not vanishing when
v\to 0
is the one for
j=m
, thus we obtain

\lim_{v\to 0}\left(\frac{1}{v}\partial_{v}\right)^{m}\alpha(u,v)=2^{m}(-1)^{m}\frac{m!}{(2m)!}\partial_{u}^{2m}(f(u)).

On the other hand we also have

\lim_{v\to 0}\left(\partial_{v}\frac{1}{v}\right)^{m}\beta(u,v)=\lim_{v\to 0}2^{m}\sum_{J=m}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}j!v^{2j-2m+1}}{(2j+1)!(j-m)!}\partial_{u}^{2j+1}(f(u))=0,

since all the terms in the summation are vanishing when
v\to 0
. Plugging these last two equations in (
6.11
) we can conclude that

\lim_{v\to 0}\Delta^{m}_{n+1}f(x)=2^{2m}(-1)^{m}m!\frac{(h_{n}-m+1)_{m}}{(2m)!}\partial_{u}^{2m}(f(u)),

where
x=u+Iv
.
∎

Proposition 6.15
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
.
Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
1\leq\alpha\leq h_{n}-1
. For
k\geq 2\alpha
we have

(6.12)

\sum_{\ell=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha}\sum_{\nu=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}\kappa_{k,h_{n},\alpha,\ell,\nu}(-2)^{\nu}=\frac{k!}{(2\alpha!)(k-2\alpha)!},

where
\kappa_{k,h_{n},\alpha,\ell,\nu}
is given in (
6.6
).

Proof.

By Corollary
6.13
, with
x=u+Iv
and formula (
3.5
) we deduce that

(6.13)

\Delta^{\alpha}_{n+1}(x^{k})\Big|_{\underline{x}=0}=k_{\alpha}\sum_{\ell=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha}\sum_{\nu=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}\kappa_{k,h_{n},\alpha,\ell,\nu}(-2)^{\nu}u^{k-2\alpha}.

By Theorem
6.14
, with
f(x)=x^{k}
, we have

\Delta^{\alpha}_{n+1}(x^{k})\Big|_{\underline{x}=0}=\lim_{v\to 0}\Delta^{\alpha}_{n+1}(x^{k})=\frac{4^{\alpha}(-1)^{\alpha}\alpha!(h_{n}-\alpha+1)_{\alpha}}{(2\alpha)!}\partial^{2\alpha}_{u}(u^{k}).

This implies that

(6.14)

\Delta^{\alpha}_{n+1}(x^{k})\Big|_{\underline{x}=0}=\frac{4^{\alpha}(-1)^{\alpha}\alpha!(h_{n}-\alpha+1)_{\alpha}}{(2\alpha)!}\frac{k!}{(k-2\alpha)!}u^{k-2\alpha}.

By putting equal (
6.13
) and (
6.14
) we obtain

k_{\alpha}\sum_{\ell=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha}\sum_{\nu=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}\kappa_{k,h_{n},\alpha,\ell,\nu}(-2)^{\nu}=\frac{4^{\alpha}(-1)^{\alpha}\alpha!(h_{n}-\alpha+1)_{\alpha}}{(2\alpha)!}\frac{k!}{(k-2\alpha)!}.

Finally the result follows by the definition of
k_{\alpha}
, see (
6.3
).
∎

Now, we provide characterization the kernel of the operator
\Delta_{n+1}^{\alpha}
.

Lemma 6.16
.

Let
n
be an odd number, set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
and let
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
1\leq\alpha\leq h_{n}-1
. If we assume that
U
is a connected slice Cauchy domain, and
f\in\mathcal{SH}_{L}(U)
(resp.
f\in\mathcal{SH}_{R}(U)
). Then
f
belongs to the kernel of the operator
\Delta_{n+1}^{\alpha}
if and only if
f(x)=\sum_{\nu=0}^{2\alpha-1}x^{\nu}\alpha_{\nu}
(resp.
f(x)=\sum_{\nu=0}^{2\alpha-1}\alpha_{\nu}x^{\nu}
), with
\{\alpha_{\nu}\}_{1\leq\nu\leq 2\alpha-1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{n}
.

Proof.

We focus on proving only the case of left slice hyperholomorphic functions, since for right slice hyperholomorphic functions the result follows from similar arguments. We proceed by double inclusion. If
f(x)=\sum_{\nu=0}^{2\alpha-1}x^{\nu}\alpha_{\nu}
it is clear that
f
belongs to the kernel of the operator
\Delta_{n+1}^{\alpha}
. Now, we suppose that
f
belongs to the kernel of
\Delta_{n+1}^{\alpha}
. Since
f
is slice hyperholomorphic we deduce that, after a change of variable if it is needed, there exists
r>0
such that the function
f
can be expanded in a convergent series at the origin

f(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}x^{k}\alpha_{k},\quad\hbox{for}\,\{\alpha_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\subset\mathbb{R}_{n},

for any
x\in B_{r}(0)
. Now, we apply the differential operator
\Delta_{n+1}^{\alpha}
and we get

(6.15)

0=\Delta_{n+1}^{\alpha}f(x)=\sum_{k=2\alpha}^{\infty}\Delta_{n+1}^{\alpha}(x^{k})\alpha_{k},\qquad\forall x\in B_{r}(0).

Now, we restrict the polynomial
\Delta_{n+1}^{\alpha}(x^{k})
to a neighbourhood of the real axis
\Omega
, by Corollary
6.13
, formula (
3.5
) and Proposition
6.15
we get

\left(\Delta^{\alpha}_{n+1}x^{k}\right)\Big|_{\underline{x}=0}=c_{k}x_{0}^{k-2\alpha},\qquad c_{k}:=\frac{4^{\alpha}(-1)^{\alpha}\alpha!(h_{n}-\alpha+1)_{\alpha}}{(2\alpha)!}\frac{k!}{(k-2\alpha)!},\qquad k\geq 2\alpha.

Thus by restricting (
6.15
) to a neighbourhood of the real axis we get

\alpha_{k}=0,\qquad\forall k\geq 2\alpha.

Therefore we have that
f(x)=\sum_{\nu=0}^{2\alpha-1}x^{\nu}\alpha_{\nu}
in
\Omega
. Finally since
U
is a connected set we get that
f(x)=\sum_{\nu=0}^{2\alpha-1}x^{\nu}\alpha_{\nu}
for any
x\in U
.
∎

Next we show that holomorphic Cliffordian function has an integral representation.

Theorem 6.17
.

Let
n
be an odd number, set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
and let
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
1\leq\alpha\leq h_{n}-1
.
Let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be a bounded slice Cauchy domain, for
I\in\mathbb{S}
, we set
ds_{I}=ds(-I)
.

•

If
f
is a left slice hyperholomorphic function on a set that contains
\overline{U}
, then the left axially holomorphic Cliffordian function
\breve{f}_{0}(x)=\Delta_{n+1}^{\alpha}f(x)
, of order
(h_{n}-\alpha,1)
, has the integral representation

(6.16)

\breve{f}_{0}(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathcal{K}^{L}_{\alpha}(s,x)ds_{I}f(s),

•

If
f
is a right slice hyperholomorphic function on a set that contains
\overline{U}
, then the right axially holomorphic Cliffordian function
\breve{f}_{0}(x)=\Delta_{n+1}^{\alpha}f(x)
, of order
(h_{n}-\alpha,1)
,
has the integral representation

(6.17)

\breve{f}_{0}(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}f(s)ds_{I}\mathcal{K}^{R}_{\alpha}(s,x).

Moreover, the integrals in (
6.16
) and (
6.17
) depend neither on
U
and nor on the imaginary unit
I\in\mathbb{S}
and not even on the kernel of
\Delta_{n+1}^{\alpha}
.

Proof.

The result follows by combining (
6.2
) and the Cauchy formula for slice hyperholomorphic functions, see Theorem
2.14
. The independence from the set
U
and the imaginary unit
I\in\mathbb{S}
follows from the Cauchy theorem, similar arguments as in Theorem
4.33
show the independence on the kernel of
\Delta_{n+1}^{\alpha}
.
∎

7.
The holomorphic Cliffordian functional calculus based on the
S
-spectrum

We illustrate
with the diagram shows the holomorphic Cliffordian fine structure that we construct in this section, the function spaces and the related functional calculi. In this section we investigate the central part of the diagram, specifically the holomorphic Cliffordian functional calculus.

\begin{CD}\mathcal{SH}_{L}(U)@>{\Delta_{n+1}^{\alpha}}>{}>\mathcal{AHC}_{h_{n}-\alpha,1}^{L}(U)@>{\Delta_{n+1}^{\alpha}}>{}>\mathcal{AM}_{L}(U)\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
S\text{-Functional calculus}\text{Holomorphic Cliff. functional calculus }F\text{-Functional calculus}\\
\end{CD}

A similar diagram holds for functions in
\mathcal{SH}_{R}(U)
.
We start with the definition of the resolvent operators for this fine structure.

Definition 7.1
.

Let
n
be an odd number, set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
and let
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
1\leq\alpha\leq h_{n}-1
.
Let
T\in\mathcal{BC}^{0,1}(V_{n})
and
s\in\rho_{S}(T)
. We define the left
\mathcal{K}
-resolvent operator as

\mathcal{K}^{L}_{\alpha}(s,T):=k_{\alpha}(s\mathcal{I}-\overline{T})\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(T)^{-\alpha-1}

and the right
\mathcal{K}
-resolvent operator as

\mathcal{K}^{R}_{\alpha}(s,T):=k_{\alpha}\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(T)^{-\alpha-1}(s\mathcal{I}-\overline{T}),

where
k_{\alpha}
is defined in (
6.3
).

Now, we write the
\mathcal{K}
-resolvent operator in terms of the
F
-resolvent operator.

Proposition 7.2
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
. For
s\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
,
T\in\mathcal{BC}^{0,1}(V_{n})
with
1\leq\alpha\leq h_{n}-1
we have

(7.1)

\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\mathcal{K}^{L}_{\alpha}(s,T)=\frac{k_{\alpha}}{\gamma_{n}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha}\sum_{\nu=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\alpha}{\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}{\nu}F_{n}^{L}(s,T)s^{h_{n}-\alpha+\ell-\nu}(-2T_{0})^{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell-\nu}|T|^{2\nu},

and

(7.2)

\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\mathcal{K}^{R}_{\alpha}(s,T)=\frac{k_{\alpha}}{\gamma_{n}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha}\sum_{\nu=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\alpha}{\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}{\nu}(-2T_{0})^{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell-\nu}|T|^{2\nu}s^{h_{n}-\alpha+\ell-\nu}F_{n}^{R}(s,T),

where
k_{\alpha}
are defined in (
6.3
) and
\gamma_{n}
are given in (
3.12
).

Proof.

We prove only (
7.1
) since (
7.2
) can be obtained by using similar results. By the definitions of the left
\mathcal{K}
-resolvent operator, the
F
-resolvent operator and the binomial theorem we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(s,T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{k_{\alpha}}{\gamma_{n}}F_{n}^{L}(s,T)\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}^{h_{n}-\alpha}(T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{k_{\alpha}}{\gamma_{n}}F_{n}^{L}(s,T)\sum_{\ell=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha}\binom{h_{n}-\alpha}{\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\alpha}{\ell}s^{2\ell}(|T|^{2}-2T_{0}s)^{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{k_{\alpha}}{\gamma_{n}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha}\sum_{\nu=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\alpha}{\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}{\nu}F_{n}^{L}(s,T)s^{h_{n}-\alpha+\ell-\nu}(-2T_{0})^{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell-\nu}|T|^{2\nu}.

∎

Proposition 7.3
.

Let
n
be an odd number, set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
and let
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
1\leq\alpha\leq h_{n}-1
.
Then, for
s\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
such that
\|T\|<|s|
,
the left
\mathcal{K}
-resolvent operator admits the series expansion

(7.3)

\mathcal{K}^{L}_{\alpha}(s,T)=k_{\alpha}\sum_{k=2\alpha}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha}\sum_{\nu=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}\kappa_{k,h_{n},\alpha,\ell,\nu}P^{n}_{k-2\alpha-2\ell+\nu}(T)(-2T_{0})^{\nu}|T|^{2(\ell-\nu)}s^{-1-k},

and, the right
\mathcal{K}
-resolvent operator admits the series expansion

(7.4)

\mathcal{K}_{R}^{\alpha}(s,T)=k_{\alpha}\sum_{k=2\alpha}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha}\sum_{\nu=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}\kappa_{k,h_{n},\alpha,\ell,\nu}s^{-1-k}P^{n}_{k-2\alpha-2\ell+\nu}(T)(-2T_{0})^{\nu}|T|^{2(\ell-\nu)},

where
k_{\alpha}
are defined in (
6.3
) and
\kappa_{k,h_{n},\alpha,\ell,\nu}
are given in (
6.6
).

Proof.

The proof follows by combining Proposition
7.2
and the expansion in series of the
F
-resolvent operator, see Proposition
3.24
.
∎

Now, we show the regularity in the variable of the left and right
\mathcal{K}
-resolvent operators.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
n
be an odd number, set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
and
T\in\mathcal{BC}^{0,1}(V_{n})
. The left (resp. right)
\mathcal{K}
-resolvent operator is a
\mathcal{B}(V_{n})
-valued left (resp. right) slice hyperholomorphic function in the variable
s
.

Proof.

We know by Proposition
3.23
that the left (resp. right)
F
-resolvent operator is a
\mathcal{B}(V_{n})
-valued left (resp. right) slice hyperholomorphic function in the variable
s
. Thus by Proposition
7.2
and the fact that the pointwise product between a left (resp. right) slice hyperholomorphic function and a slice intrinsic hyperholomorphic function is left (resp. right) slice hyperholomorphic we get the result.
∎

Definition 7.5

(Holomorphic Cliffordian functional calculus for bounded operators)
.

Let
n
be an odd number, set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
and let
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
1\leq\alpha\leq h_{n}-1
.
Let us consider
T\in\mathcal{BC}^{1}(V_{n})
be such that its components
T_{i}
,
i=1,...,n
have real spectra. Let
U
be a bounded slice Cauchy domain as in Definition
2.22
.
We set
ds_{I}=(-I)ds
where
I\in\mathbb{S}
.

•

Then for any
f\in\mathcal{SH}_{\sigma_{S}(T)}^{L}(U)
and
\breve{f}_{0}(x)=\Delta_{n+1}^{\alpha}f(x)
we define the holomorphic Cliffordian functional calculus for bounded operators as

(7.5)

\breve{f}_{0}(T)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathcal{K}^{L}_{\alpha}(s,T)ds_{I}f(s).

•

For any
f\in\mathcal{SH}_{\sigma_{S}(T)}^{R}(U)
and
\breve{f}_{0}(x)=\Delta_{n+1}^{\alpha}f(x)
we define the holomorphic Cliffordian functional calculus for bounded operators as

(7.6)

\breve{f}_{0}(T)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}f(s)ds_{I}\mathcal{K}^{R}_{\alpha}(s,T).

Theorem 7.6
.

Let
n
be an odd number, set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
and let
\alpha
be such that
1\leq\alpha\leq h_{n}-1
.
Let us consider
T\in\mathcal{BC}^{1}(V_{n})
be such that its components
T_{i}
,
i=1,...,n
have real spectra. Let
U
be a bounded slice Cauchy domain as in Definition
2.22
.
We set
ds_{I}=(-I)ds
where
I\in\mathbb{S}
.
Then for any
f\in\mathcal{SH}_{\sigma_{S}(T)}^{L}(U)
(resp.
f\in\mathcal{SH}_{\sigma_{S}(T)}^{R}(U)
) the integral (
7.5
) (resp (
7.6
)) is independent of the slice Cauchy domain
U
and the imaginary unit
I\in\mathbb{S}
.

Proof.

The result follows by using similar arguments of Theorem
5.10
. Indeed also in this case the resolvent operator is left (resp. right) slice hyperholomorphic in
s
, see Lemma
7.4
, and
\lim_{s\to\infty}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}^{L}(s,T)=\lim_{s\to\infty}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}^{R}(s,T)=0.

∎

Owing to non-commutativity, the right and left forms of the holomorphic Cliffordian-functional calculus typically result in different operators. The sole exception arises when the F-functional calculus is applied to intrinsic slice hyperholomorphic functions.

Proposition 7.7
.

Let
n
be an odd number, set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
and let
\alpha
be such that
1\leq\alpha\leq h_{n}-1
. Suppose
T\in\mathcal{BC}^{0,1}(V_{n})
and
f\in\mathcal{N}_{\sigma_{S}(T)}(U)
, where
U
is a bounded slice Cauchy domain as specified in Definition
2.22
.
Then we have

\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathcal{K}^{L}_{\alpha}(s,T)\,ds_{I}\,f(s)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}f(s)ds_{I}\mathcal{K}^{R}_{\alpha}(s,T).

Proof.

The result follows by using similar arguments used to prove Theorem
3.27
.
∎

Before to show that the holomorphic Cliffordian functional calculus is independent from the kernel of the operator
\Delta_{n+1}^{\alpha}
we need the following technical result.

Theorem 7.8
.

Let
n
be an odd number, set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
and let
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
1\leq\alpha\leq h_{n}-1
.
Let
T\in\mathcal{BC}^{1}(V_{n})
be such that its components
T_{i}
,
i=1,...,n
have real spectra. Let
G
be a bounded slice Cauchy domain such that
\partial G\cap\sigma_{S}(T)=\emptyset
. Thus, for every
I\in\mathbb{S}
, we have

\int_{\partial(G\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}s^{\beta}ds_{I}\mathcal{K}^{R}_{\alpha}(s,T)=\int_{\partial(G\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathcal{K}^{L}_{\alpha}(s,T)ds_{I}s^{\beta}=0,\qquad\hbox{if}\quad 0\leq\beta\leq 2\alpha-1.

Proof.

The first equality follows from the fact that
s^{\beta}
is slice intrinsic hyperholomorphic, see Theorem
7.7
. Now we prove the first equality. By the Fueter-Sce theorem, see Theorem
3.19
, we have

(7.7)

\int_{\partial(G\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}F_{n}^{L}(s,x)ds_{J}s^{m}=\Delta^{h_{n}}_{n+1}(x^{m})=0,\qquad\hbox{if}\quad m\leq 2h_{n}-1,

for all
x\notin\partial G
and
I\in\mathbb{S}
. By Proposition
3.42
and Proposition
7.2
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\partial(G\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathcal{K}^{L}_{\alpha}(s,T)ds_{J}s^{\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{k_{\alpha}}{\gamma_{n}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha}\sum_{\nu=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\alpha}{\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}{\nu}(-2T_{0})^{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell-\nu}|T|^{2\nu}\times

\displaystyle\times\int_{\partial(G\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}F_{n}^{L}(s,T)\,ds_{I}\,s^{h_{n}-\alpha+\ell-\nu+\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{k_{\alpha}}{\gamma_{n}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha}\sum_{\nu=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\alpha}{\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}{\nu}(-2T_{0})^{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell-\nu}|T|^{2\nu}\times

\displaystyle\times\int_{\partial(G\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\left(\int_{\partial W}G(\omega,T)D\omega F_{n}^{L}(s,\omega)\right)\,ds_{I}\,s^{h_{n}-\alpha+\ell-\nu+\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{k_{\alpha}}{\gamma_{n}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha}\sum_{\nu=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\alpha}{\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}{\nu}(-2T_{0})^{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell-\nu}|T|^{2\nu}\times

\displaystyle\times\int_{\partial W}G(\omega,T)D\omega\left(\int_{\partial(G\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}F_{n}^{L}(s,\omega)\,ds_{I}\,s^{h_{n}-\alpha+\ell-\nu+\beta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

where
W
as in Definition
3.33
. The last equality holds to be true by (
7.7
), since
h_{n}-\alpha+\ell-\nu+\beta\leq 2h_{n}-1
.
∎

Proposition 7.9
.

Let
n
be an odd number, set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
and let
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
1\leq\alpha\leq h_{n}-1
. We assume
T\in\mathcal{BC}^{1}(V_{n})
be such that its components
T_{i}
,
i=1,...,n
have real spectra. Let
U
be a slice Cauchy domain with
\sigma_{S}(T)\subset U
.
Suppose that
f
,
g\in\mathcal{SH}_{\sigma_{S}(T)}^{L}(U)
(resp.
f
,
g\in\mathcal{SH}_{\sigma_{S}(T)}^{R}(U)
) and
\Delta_{n+1}^{\alpha}f(x)=\Delta_{n+1}^{\alpha}g(x)
(resp.
\Delta_{n+1}^{\alpha}f(x)=\Delta_{n+1}^{\alpha}g(x)
). Then, we have
\breve{f}_{0}(T)=\breve{g}_{0}(T)
.

Proof.

We show the result only for left slice hyperholomorphic functions, the case for right slice hyperholomorphic functions follows by using similar arguments. We start assuming that
U
is connected. By the definition of the holomorphic Cliffordian functional calculus, see Definition
7.5
, we have

\breve{f}_{0}(T)-\breve{g}_{0}(T)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathcal{K}^{L}_{\alpha}(s,T)\,ds_{I}(f(s)-g(s)).

By Lemma
7.4
we know that the operator
\mathcal{K}_{L}^{\alpha}(s,T)
is a right slice hyperholomorphic function in
s
, thus we can change the domain of integration to

\partial(B_{r}(0)\cap\mathbb{C}_{J})
for
r>0
such that
\|T\|<r
. We know by hypothesis that
f-g
belongs to the kernel of the operator
\Delta_{n+1}^{\alpha}
, thus by Lemma
6.16
we get

\displaystyle\breve{f}_{0}(T)-\breve{g}_{0}(T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(B_{r}(0)\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathcal{K}^{L}_{\alpha}(s,T)ds_{I}(f(s)-g(s))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{\nu=0}^{2\alpha-1}\int_{\partial(B_{r}(0)\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathcal{K}^{L}_{\alpha}(s,T)\,ds_{I}s^{\nu}\alpha_{\nu}

where
\{\alpha_{\nu}\}_{1\leq\nu\leq 2\alpha-1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{n}
. By Theorem
7.8
(since
\nu\leq 2\alpha-1
) the previous integral is zero.

Now, we suppose taht
U
is not connected so we can write
U=\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{m}U_{\ell}
where
U_{\ell}
are the connected components of the set
U
. Thus by Lemma
6.16
we have
f(s)-g(s)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\sum_{\nu=0}^{2\alpha-1}\chi_{U_{\ell}}s^{\nu}\alpha_{\nu,\ell}
. Hence by the definition of the Cliffordian functional calculus, see Definition
7.5
, we have

\breve{f}_{0}(T)-\breve{g}_{0}(T)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\sum_{\nu=0}^{2\alpha-1}\int_{\partial(U_{\ell}\cap\mathbb{C}_{J})}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}^{L}(s,T)ds_{J}s^{\nu}\alpha_{\nu,\ell}.

Finally, by Theorem
7.8
we get that
\breve{f}_{0}(T)=\breve{g}_{0}(T)
.
∎

Now, we show some algebraic properties of the holomorphic Cliffordian functional calculus.

Lemma 7.10
.

Let
n
be an odd number, set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
and let
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
1\leq\alpha\leq h_{n}-1
.
We assume
T\in\mathcal{BC}^{1}(V_{n})
be such its components
T_{i}
,
i=1,...,n
have real spectra.
Suppose that
\breve{f}_{0}(x)=\Delta_{n+1}^{\alpha}f(x)
and
\breve{g}_{0}(x)=\Delta_{n+1}^{\alpha}g(x)

with
f
,
g\in\mathcal{SH}_{\sigma_{S}(T)}^{L}(U)
(resp.
\mathcal{SH}_{\sigma_{S}(T)}^{R}(U)
) and let
a\in\mathbb{R}_{n}
.
Then, we have

(\breve{f}_{0}a+\breve{g}_{0})(T)=\breve{f}_{0}(T)a+\breve{g}_{0}(T),\qquad(resp.(a\breve{f}_{0}+\breve{g}_{0})(T)=a\breve{f}_{0}(T)+\breve{g}_{0}(T)).

Proof.

The result follows from the linearity of the integrals in Definition
7.5
.
∎

Proposition 7.11
.

Let
n
be an odd number, set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
and let
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
1\leq\alpha\leq h_{n}-1
.
We assume
T\in\mathcal{BC}^{1}(V_{n})
be such that its components
T_{i}
,
i=1,...,n
have real spectra.

•

Let
\breve{f}_{0}(x)=\Delta_{n+1}^{\alpha}f(x)

where
f(x)=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}x^{m}a_{m}
with
a_{m}\in\mathbb{R}_{n}
, converges on a ball
B_{r}(0)
, for
r>0
, with
\sigma_{S}(T)\subset B_{r}(0)
.
Then, we have

\breve{f}_{0}(T)=\frac{k_{\alpha}}{\gamma_{n}}\sum_{k=2\alpha}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha}\sum_{\nu=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}\kappa_{k,h_{n},\alpha,\ell,\nu}P^{n}_{k-2\alpha-2\ell+\nu}(T)(-2T_{0})^{\nu}|T|^{2(\ell-\nu)}a_{k}.

•

Let
\breve{f}_{0}(x)=\Delta_{n+1}^{\alpha}f(x)
where
f(x)=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}a_{m}x^{m}
with
a_{m}\in\mathbb{R}_{n}
, converges on a ball
B_{r}(0)
, for
r>0
, with
\sigma_{S}(T)\subset B_{r}(0)
. Then, we have

\breve{f}_{0}(T)=\frac{k_{\alpha}}{\gamma_{n}}\sum_{k=2\alpha}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha}\sum_{\nu=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}a_{k}\kappa_{k,h_{n},\alpha,\ell,\nu}P^{n}_{k-2\alpha-2\ell+\nu}(T)(-2T_{0})^{\nu}|T|^{2(\ell-\nu)}.

Proof.

We prove only the first statement since the second one follows from similar arguments. We pick an imaginary
I\in\mathbb{S}
and a radius
0<R<r
such that
\sigma_{S}(T)\subset B_{R}(0)
. Thus the series expansion of
f
converges uniformly on
\partial(B_{R}(0)\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})
, and so we have

\displaystyle\breve{f}_{0}(T)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(B_{R}(0)\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathcal{K}^{L}_{\alpha}(s,T)ds_{I}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}s^{m}a_{m}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\int_{\partial(B_{R}(0)\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathcal{K}^{L}_{\alpha}(s,T)ds_{I}s^{m}a_{m}.

By using the series expansion of the resolvent operator
\mathcal{K}^{L}_{\alpha}(s,T)
obtained in Proposition
7.3
we have

\displaystyle\breve{f}_{0}(T)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{k_{\alpha}}{\gamma_{n}}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=2\alpha}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha}\sum_{\nu=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}\kappa_{k,h_{n},\alpha,\ell,\nu}P^{n}_{k-2\alpha-2\ell+\nu}(T)(-2T_{0})^{\nu}|T|^{2(\ell-\nu)}

\displaystyle\times\int_{\partial(B_{R}(0)\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}s^{-1-k+m}a_{m}ds_{I}.

Now, by using the fact that

\int_{\partial(B_{R}(0)\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}s^{-1-k+m}ds_{I}=\begin{cases}2\pi,\qquad\hbox{if}\quad m=k\\
0,\qquad\hbox{if}\quad m\neq k,\end{cases}

we get

\breve{f}_{0}(T)=\frac{k_{\alpha}}{\gamma_{n}}\sum_{k=2\alpha}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha}\sum_{\nu=0}^{h_{n}-\alpha-\ell}\kappa_{k,h_{n},\alpha,\ell,\nu}P^{n}_{k+2\ell+\nu-2h_{n}}(T)(-2T_{0})^{\nu}|T|^{2h_{n}-2\alpha-2\ell-2\nu}a_{k}.

This proves the result.
∎

7.1.
Product rule for the
F
-functional calculus

In order to get a product formula for the
F
-functional calculus we need to use the polyharmonic functional calculus and the holomorphic Cliffordian functional calculus developed in the previous sections. We need the following preliminary result, see
[
8
, Lemma 3.18]
.

Lemma 7.12
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
B\in\mathcal{B}(V_{n})
. Let
G
be an axially symmetric domain and assume that
f\in\mathcal{N}(G)
. Then, for
p\in G
, we have

\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(G\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}f(s)ds_{I}(\bar{s}B-Bp)(p^{2}-2s_{0}p+|s|^{2})^{-1}=Bf(p).

Theorem 7.13

(Product formula for the
F
-functional calculus)
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
.
We assume
T\in\mathcal{BC}^{1}(V_{n})
be such that its components
T_{i}
,
i=1,...,n
have real spectra.
Suppose that
f\in\mathcal{N}^{L}_{\sigma_{S}(T)}(U)
and
g\in\mathcal{SH}^{L}_{\sigma_{S}(T)}(U)
. Then, we have

(7.8)

\displaystyle\Delta^{h_{n}}_{n+1}(fg)(T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\Delta^{h_{n}}_{n+1}f)(T)g(T)+f(T)(\Delta^{h_{n}}_{n+1}g)(T)+\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-2}(\Delta^{h_{n}-i-1}_{n+1}f)(T)(\Delta_{n+1}^{i+1}g)(T)

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-1}(D\Delta^{h_{n}-i-1}_{n+1}f)(T)(D\Delta^{i}_{n+1}g)(T).

Proof.

We consider
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
two bounded slice Cauchy domains such that contain
\sigma_{S}(T)
and
\overline{G}_{1}\subset G_{2}
, with
\overline{G}_{2}\subset\hbox{dom}(f)\cap\hbox{dom}(g)
. For every
I\in\mathbb{S}
we pick
p\in\partial(G_{1}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})
and
s\in\partial(G_{2}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})
. Then by the definition of the
S
-functional calculus (see Definition
2.23
),
F
-functional calculus (see Definition
3.25
), the polyharmonic functional calculus (see Definition
5.8
) and the holomorphic Cliffordian functional calculus (see Definition
7.5
) we have

(7.9)

\displaystyle(\Delta^{h_{n}}_{n+1}f)(T)g(T)+f(T)(\Delta^{h_{n}}_{n+1}g)(T)+\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-2}(\Delta^{h_{n}-i-1}_{n+1}f)(T)(\Delta^{i+1}_{n+1}g)(T)

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-1}(D\Delta^{h_{n}-i-1}_{n+1}f)(T)(D\Delta^{i}_{n+1}g)(T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{\partial(G_{2}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}f(s)ds_{I}F_{n}^{R}(s,T)\int_{\partial(G_{1}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}S^{-1}_{L}(p,T)dp_{I}g(p)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{\partial(G_{2}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}f(s)ds_{I}S^{-1}_{R}(s,T)\int_{\partial(G_{1}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}F_{n}^{L}(p,T)dp_{I}g(p)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-2}\int_{\partial(G_{2}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}f(s)ds_{I}\mathcal{K}^{R}_{h_{n}-i-1}(s,T)\int_{\partial(G_{1}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathcal{K}^{L}_{i+1}(p,T)dp_{I}g(p)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-1}\int_{\partial(G_{2}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\sigma_{n,h_{n}-i}\sigma_{n,i+1}f(s)ds_{I}\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}(T)^{-h_{n}+i}\int_{\partial(G_{1}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathcal{Q}_{c,p}(T)^{-i-1}dp_{I}g(p),

where we have also used Proposition
2.25
, Proposition
3.27
, Proposition
7.7
and Proposition
5.11
.
From the definition of the left and right
\mathcal{K}
-resolvent operators, see Definition
7.1
, we have

\mathcal{K}_{R}^{h_{n}-i-1}(s,T)=k_{h_{n}-i-1}\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}^{-h_{n}+i}(T)(s\mathcal{I}-\overline{T})=k_{h_{n}-i-1}\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}^{-h_{n}+i+1}(T)S^{-1}_{R}(s,T),

and

\mathcal{K}_{L}^{i+1}(p,T)=k_{i+1}(p\mathcal{I}-\overline{T})\mathcal{Q}_{c,p}^{-i-2}(T)=k_{i+1}S^{-1}_{L}(p,T)\mathcal{Q}_{c,p}^{-i-1}(T).

Now, by (
4.31
) and (
3.12
), we have

k_{h_{n}-i-1}k_{i+1}=\gamma_{n}
and
\sigma_{n,h_{n}-i}\sigma_{n,i+1}=-\gamma_{n}
, where the constants
k_{\alpha}
,
\gamma_{n}
and
\sigma_{n,\ell}
are defined
in (
6.3
), (
3.12
) and(
4.31
), respectively.
Hence we can write (
7.9
) as

(7.10)

\displaystyle(\Delta^{h_{n}}_{n+1}f)(T)g(T)+f(T)(\Delta^{h_{n}}_{n+1}g)(T)+\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-2}(\Delta^{h_{n}-i-1}_{n+1}f)(T)(\Delta^{i+1}_{n+1}g)(T)

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-1}(D\Delta^{h_{n}-i-1}_{n+1}f)(T)(D\Delta^{i}_{n+1}g)(T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{\partial(G_{2}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\int_{\partial(G_{1}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}f(s)ds_{I}\Big\{F_{n}^{R}(s,T)S^{-1}_{L}(p,T)+S^{-1}_{R}(s,T)F_{n}^{L}(p,T)

\displaystyle+\gamma_{n}\Big[\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-2}\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}^{-h_{n}+i+1}(T)S^{-1}_{R}(s,T)S^{-1}_{L}(p,T)\mathcal{Q}_{c,p}^{-i-1}(T)+\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-1}\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}^{-h_{n}+i}(T)\mathcal{Q}_{c,p}^{-i-1}(T)\Big]\Big\}dp_{I}g(p).

Now, by using Theorem
3.28
we can write (
7.10
) as

(7.11)

\displaystyle(\Delta^{h_{n}}_{n+1}f)(T)g(T)+f(T)(\Delta^{h_{n}}_{n+1}g)(T)+\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-2}(\Delta^{h_{n}-i-1}_{n+1}f)(T)(\Delta^{i+1}_{n+1}g)(T)

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-1}(D\Delta^{h_{n}-i-1}_{n+1}f)(T)(D\Delta^{i}_{n+1}g)(T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{\partial(G_{2}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\int_{\partial(G_{1}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}f(s)ds_{I}\left[\left(F_{n}^{R}(s,T)-F_{n}^{L}(p,T)\right)p\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\bar{s}\left(F_{n}^{R}(s,T)-F_{n}^{L}(p,T)\right)\right](p^{2}-2s_{0}p+|s|^{2})^{-1}.

By using the linearity of the integrals we get

\displaystyle(\Delta^{h_{n}}_{n+1}f)(T)g(T)+f(T)(\Delta^{h_{n}}_{n+1}g)(T)+\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-2}(\Delta^{h_{n}-i-1}_{n+1}f)(T)(\Delta^{i+1}_{n+1}g)(T)

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-1}(D\Delta^{h_{n}-i-1}_{n+1}f)(T)(D\Delta^{i}_{n+1}g)(T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{\partial(G_{2}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}f(s)ds_{I}\int_{\partial(G_{1}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}F_{n}^{R}(s,T)p(p^{2}-2s_{0}p+|s|^{2})^{-1}dp_{I}g(p)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{\partial(G_{2}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}f(s)ds_{I}\int_{\partial(G_{1}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}F_{n}^{L}(s,T)p(p^{2}-2s_{0}p+|s|^{2})^{-1}dp_{I}g(p)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{\partial(G_{2}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}f(s)ds_{I}\int_{\partial(G_{1}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\bar{s}F_{n}^{R}(s,T)(p^{2}-2s_{0}p+|s|^{2})^{-1}dp_{I}g(p)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{\partial(G_{2}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}f(s)ds_{I}\int_{\partial(G_{1}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\bar{s}F_{n}^{L}(s,T)(p^{2}-2s_{0}p+|s|^{2})^{-1}dp_{I}g(p).

Now, we observe that the functions
p\mapsto p(p^{2}-2s_{0}p+|s|^{2})^{-1}
,
p\mapsto(p^{2}-2s_{0}p+|s|^{2})^{-1}
are intrinsic slice hyperholomorphic on
G_{1}
, by Theorem
2.8
, we get

\int_{\partial(G_{2}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}f(s)ds_{I}\int_{\partial(G_{1}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}F_{n}^{R}(s,T)p(p^{2}-2s_{0}p+|s|^{2})^{-1}dp_{I}g(p)=0,

\int_{\partial(G_{2}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}f(s)ds_{I}\int_{\partial(G_{1}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\bar{s}F_{n}^{L}(s,T)(p^{2}-2s_{0}p+|s|^{2})^{-1}dp_{I}g(p)=0.

Thus we obtain

\displaystyle(\Delta^{h_{n}}_{n+1}f)(T)g(T)+f(T)(\Delta^{h_{n}}_{n+1}g)(T)+\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-2}(\Delta^{h_{n}-i-1}_{n+1}f)(T)(\Delta^{i+1}_{n+1}g)(T)

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-1}(D\Delta^{h_{n}-i-1}_{n+1}f)(T)(D\Delta^{i}_{n+1}g)(T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{\partial(G_{1}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\int_{\partial(G_{2}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}f(s)ds_{I}\left[\bar{s}F_{n}^{L}(p,T)-F_{n}^{L}(p,T)p\right](p^{2}-2s_{0}p+|s|^{2})^{-1}dp_{I}g(p).

Finally by applying Lemma
7.12
with
B:=F_{n}^{L}(p,T)
and the definition of the
F
-functional calculus, see Definition
3.25
, we have

\displaystyle(\Delta^{h_{n}}_{n+1}f)(T)g(T)+f(T)(\Delta^{h_{n}}_{n+1}g)(T)+\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-2}(\Delta^{h_{n}-i-1}f)(T)(\Delta^{i+1}_{n+1}g)(T)

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-1}(D\Delta^{h_{n}-i-1}_{n+1}f)(T)(D\Delta^{i}_{n+1}g)(T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(G_{1}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}F_{n}^{L}(p,T)dp_{I}f(p)g(p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta^{h_{n}}_{n+1}(fg).

This proves the result.
∎

Remark 7.14
.

If we consider
n=3
in formula (
7.8
) we get back to the formula obtained in
[
32
, Thm. 9.3 ]
, since we understood the sum
\sum_{i=0}^{-1}
to be zero. Furthermore, if we consider
n=5
we get back exactly the same formula of
[
33
, Them. 10.3]
.

Remark 7.15
.

By replacing the operator
T
with the paravector
x
in (
7.8
), and setting
f(x)=x

and
g(x)=x^{m}
, with
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we re-obtain formula (
4.36
).

Remark 7.16
.

To get the product formula for the
F
-functional calculus it is crucial the holomorphic Cliffordian functional calculus developed in this section.

8.
Integral representation of polyanalytic functions

The function spaces that we have considered in the previous sections, i.e. slice hyperholomorphic, axially polyharmonic and axially monogenic functions appear in many fields of pure and applied mathematics, the polyanalytic functions are less known but still have several applications.

The complex version of this class of functions was first considered by G.V. Kolossov in connection with his research on elasticity and also have applications in signal analysis, particularly in the context of Gabor frames with Hermite functions, as shown by the results of Gröchenig and Lyubarskii. Polyanalytic functions provide explicit representation formulas for functions in the eigenspaces of the Euclidean Laplacian with a magnetic field, which are referred to as Landau levels. This likely has significant implications in quantum mechanics and related fields. For an overview of the applications of this class of functions see the paper
[
2
]
and also
[
1
,
16
,
79
,
87
]
for further material on this theory.
Finally, we remark that the study and development of polyanalytic boundary value problems have continued to flourish in recent times. For more details and further developments, the reader can refer to the papers
[
18
,
19
,
20
,
80
,
82
,
63
]
and the references therein.

A polyanalytic function belongs to the kernel of the powers of the Dirac operator and where first considered by F. Brackx in
[
22
]
.

Definition 8.1
.

Let
m\geq 1
. We assume that
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
is an open set and let
f:U\to\mathbb{R}_{n}
be a function of class
\mathcal{C}^{m}(U)
and is of axial type (see (
3.1
)). We say that
f
is left (resp. right) axially polyanalytic (of order
m
) on
U
if

D^{m}f(x)=0,\qquad\forall x\in U\qquad\left(\hbox{resp. }\quad f(x)D^{m}=0,\qquad\forall x\in U\right).

One of the peculiarity of an axially polyanalytic function is that they can be decomposed in terms of axially monogenic functions.

Theorem 8.2
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be an open set. A function
f:U\to\mathbb{R}_{n}
is left (resp. right) axially polyanalytic of order
m
if and only if it can be decomposed in terms of unique left (resp. right) axially monogenic functions
f_{0}
,…,
f_{m}
such that

f(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}x_{0}^{k}f_{k}(x).

Now, we show that the class of left and right axially polyanalytic function arise naturally in the factorization of the second map
T_{FS2}
of the Fueter-Sce extension theorem.

In this section, we will establish each result exclusively for left slice hyperholomorphic functions, since similar arguments can be used to obtain the corresponding results for right slice hyperholomorphic functions.

Theorem 8.3
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
assume that
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
is such that
0\leq\ell\leq h_{n}-1
. Then, the action of the operator

T_{FS}^{(III)}=\Delta_{n+1}^{\ell}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}

on a left (resp. right) slice hyperholomorphic function on an axially symmetric open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
gives a left (resp. right) axially polyanalytic function of order
h_{n}-\ell+1
.

Proof.

Let
f
be a left slice hyperholomorphic function. First we show that
T_{FS}^{(III)}f(x)
is of axial form. By Lemma
4.7
we get that
\Delta_{n+1}^{\ell}f(x)
is of axial type. Finally by Proposition
6.4
we obtain that also
T_{FS}^{(III)}f(x)
is of axial type. Now, we have to show that

D^{h_{n}-\ell+1}T_{FS}^{(III)}f(x)=0,\ \ \ \forall x\in U.

By the Fueter-Sce mapping theorem, see Theorem
3.13
and the fact that
\Delta_{n+1}=D\overline{D}
we have

\displaystyle D^{h_{n}-\ell+1}\left(T_{FS}^{(III)}f(x)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D^{h_{n}-\ell+1}\left(\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}f(x)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D^{h_{n}-\ell+1}\left(D^{\ell}\overline{D}^{\ell}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}f(x)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D\Delta^{h_{n}}_{n+1}f(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,\ \ \ \forall x\in U.

∎

Definition 8.4

(Axially polyanalytic functions associated with
\mathcal{SH}_{L}(U)
and
\mathcal{SH}_{R}(U)
)
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
. We define left axially polyanalytic functions
of order
h_{n}-\ell+1
, where
0\leq\ell\leq h_{n}-1
, as

\mathcal{AP}_{h_{n}-\ell+1}^{L}(U)=\{\Delta_{n+1}^{\ell}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}f\ :\ f\in\mathcal{SH}_{L}(U)\}.

We define right axially polyanalytic functions
of order
h_{n}-\ell+1
, where
0\leq\ell\leq h_{n}-1
, as

\mathcal{AP}_{h_{n}-\ell+1}^{R}(U)=\{f\Delta_{n+1}^{\ell}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}\ :\ f\in\mathcal{SH}_{R}(U)\}.

Remark 8.5
.

Theorem
8.3
give the following diagram:

(8.1)

\begin{CD}&&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{SH}_{L}(U)}@>{\ \ \overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}}>{}>{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{AP}_{h_{n}-\ell+1}^{L}(U)}@>{\ \ D^{h_{n}-\ell}}>{}>{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{AM}_{L}(U)},\end{CD}

and similarly for the case
\mathcal{SH}_{R}(U)
.

Remark 8.6
.

The diagram in Remark
8.5
can be further decomposed as follows:

(8.2)

\begin{CD}&&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{SH}_{L}(U)}@>{\ \ \overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}}>{}>{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{AP}_{h_{n}-\ell+1}^{L}(U)}@>{\ \ D}>{}>{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{AP}_{h_{n}-\ell}^{L}(U)}@>{\ \ D}>{}>{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{AP}_{h_{n}-\ell-1}^{L}(U)}\\
&&@>{\ \ D}>{}>{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}...}@>{\ \ D}>{}>{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{AP}_{2}^{L}(U)}@>{\ \ D^{2}}>{}>{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{AM}_{L}(U)}.\end{CD}

Similarly to what we did for the axially polyharmonic and holomorphic Cliffordian functions, our goal is to provide an integral representation of axially polyanalytic functions. To address this problem, we need to apply the operator
T_{FS}^{(III)}
to the left slice hyperholomorphic Cauchy kernel. However, this is a challenging problem, whose solution needs some preliminary results. To avoid overburdening the main text, we provide a detailed proof of the following result in the appendix, see Section 10.
In Proposition
8.7
we concentrate on the case of left slice hyperholomorphic function,
but this result can be re-adapted for the right slice hyperholomorphic setting as well.

Proposition 8.7
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
n
be a fixed odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
. We assume that
s
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
are such that
s\notin[x]
. If
\beta=2m+1
, we have

\displaystyle\overline{D}^{\beta}S^{-1}_{L}(s,x)=\frac{2^{2m+1}}{(h_{n}-2m-2)!}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{m}2^{2j}(m+j)!(h_{n}-m-j-1)!\binom{m+j}{2j}(s-x_{0})^{2j}\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}^{-m-j-1}(x)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+(s-\overline{x})\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}2^{2j+1}(m+j+1)!(h_{n}-m-j-2)!\binom{m+j+1}{2j+1}(s-x_{0})^{2j+1}\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}^{-m-j-2}(x)\right)

(8.3)

\displaystyle+4^{2m+1}(2m+1)!(s-\overline{x})(s-x_{0})^{2m+1}\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}^{-2m-2}(x).

If
\beta=2m
, we have

(8.4)

\displaystyle\overline{D}^{\beta}S^{-1}_{L}(s,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2^{2m}}{(h_{n}-2m-1)!}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}2^{2j+1}(m+j)!(h_{n}-m-j-1)!\binom{m+j}{2j+1}\right.

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times(s-x_{0})^{2j+1}\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}^{-m-j-1}(x)

\displaystyle\left.+(s-\overline{x})\sum_{j=0}^{m-2}2^{2j}(m+j)!(h_{n}-m-j-1)!\binom{m+j}{2j}(s-x_{0})^{2j}\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}^{-m-j-1}(x)\right)

\displaystyle+4^{2m}(2m)!(s-\overline{x})(s-x_{0})^{2m}\mathcal{Q}_{c,s}^{-2m-1}(x).

The above result is crucial for proving the following statement.

Proposition 8.8
.

Let
n
be a fixed odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
. For
s
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
such that
s\notin[x]
we have

\overline{D}^{h_{n}}S^{-1}_{L}(s,x)=\frac{(-1)^{h_{n}}}{h_{n}!}F_{n}^{L}(s,x)(s-x_{0})^{h_{n}}.

Proof.

Recall that the left
F
-resolvent kernel, defined in (
3.14
), written in terms of the Sce exponent
h_{n}
is

(8.5)

F_{n}^{L}(s,x):=\gamma_{n}(s-\bar{x})(s^{2}-2{\rm Re}(x)s+|x|^{2})^{-h_{n}-1},

where
\gamma_{n}
are defined as (
3.12
), i.e.,

\gamma_{n}:=\left[\Gamma\left(\frac{n+1}{2}\right)\right]^{2}2^{n-1}(-1)^{\frac{n-1}{2}}.

Recall that by assumption
n
be an odd number as a consequence
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
are natural numbers.
So, to apply Proposition
8.7
, we have to distinguish the cases where
h_{n}
is even or odd.
With this observation the proof is a consequence
of formulas (
8.3
) and (
8.4
), once it is observed that the factors:

\frac{(h_{n}-m-j-2)!}{(h_{n}-2m-2)!}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \frac{(h_{n}-m-j-1)!}{(h_{n}-2m-2)!}

in (
8.3
) and

\frac{(h_{n}-m-j-1)!}{(h_{n}-2m-1)!}

in (
8.4
) are zero if
\beta=h_{n}
.
∎

Remark 8.9
.

One might inquire why Proposition
8.8
was not proven using induction on
h_{n}
. The issue with employing such a method lies in the fact that, in the inductive step, the imaginary units of
\overline{D}
increase due to their dependence on
h_{n}
. Consequently, it is as issue to apply the induction principle directly.

Theorem 8.10
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
assume that
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
is such that
0\leq\ell\leq h_{n}-1
. For
s
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
with
s\notin[x]
, we have

(8.6)

\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}S^{-1}_{L}(s,x)=\frac{(-1)^{\ell-h_{n}}}{(h_{n}-\ell)!}F_{n}^{L}(s,x)(s-x_{0})^{h_{n}-\ell},

and

S^{-1}_{R}(s,x)\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}=\frac{(-1)^{\ell-h_{n}}}{(h_{n}-\ell)!}(s-x_{0})^{h_{n}-\ell}F_{n}^{R}(s,x).

Proof.

We prove the result by induction on
\ell
. For
\ell=0
formula (
8.6
) follows by Proposition
8.8
. We suppose that statement is true for
\ell
and we prove it for
\ell+1
. By the induction principle we have

\displaystyle\Delta^{\ell+1}_{n+1}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell-1}S^{-1}_{L}(s,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D^{\ell+1}\overline{D}^{h_{n}}S^{-1}_{L}(s,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{\ell-h_{n}}}{(h_{n}-\ell)!}D\left(F_{n}^{L}(s,x)(s-x_{0})^{h_{n}-\ell}\right).

By using the Leibniz formula for the operator
D
, since
(s-x_{0})^{h_{n}-\ell}
is real-valued in
x
, and the fact that the kernel
F_{n}^{L}(s,x)
is axially monogenic in
x
, see Proposition
6.11
, we get

\displaystyle\Delta^{\ell+1}_{n+1}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell-1}S^{-1}_{L}(s,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{\ell-h_{n}}}{(h_{n}-\ell)!}F_{n}^{L}(s,x)D(s-x_{0})^{h_{n}-\ell}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{\ell-h_{n}+1}}{(h_{n}-\ell-1)!}F_{n}^{L}(s,x)(s-x_{0})^{h_{n}-\ell-1}.

This proves the result.
∎

Definition 8.11

(Axially polyanalytic kernels)
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
assume that
\ell
is such that
0\leq\ell\leq h_{n}-1
. For
s
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
such that
s\notin[x]
.
We define the left and right axially polyanalytic kernels of order
h_{n}-\ell+1
as

\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\ell}(s,x):=\frac{(-1)^{\ell-h_{n}}}{(h_{n}-\ell)!}F_{n}^{L}(s,x)(s-x_{0})^{h_{n}-\ell},\quad\hbox{and}\quad\mathcal{P}^{R}_{\ell}(s,x)=\frac{(-1)^{\ell-h_{n}}}{(h_{n}-\ell)!}(s-x_{0})^{h_{n}-\ell}F_{n}^{R}(s,x).

The above definition is motivated by the following proposition.

Proposition 8.12
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
assume that
\ell
is such that
0\leq\ell\leq h_{n}-1
.
Assume that
s
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, with
s\notin[x]
.
Then, we have that
\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\ell}(s,x)
(resp.
\mathcal{P}^{R}_{\ell}(s,x)
) is left (resp. right) intrinsic hyperholomorphic in the variable
s
and left (resp. right) axially polyanalytic of order
h_{n}-\ell+1
in
x
.

Proof.

Since the kernel
F_{n}^{L}(s,x)
(resp.
F_{n}^{R}(s,x)
) is left (resp, right) in the variable
s
, see Proposition
3.16
, and the function
(s-x_{0})^{h_{n}-\ell}
is slice intrinsic we get that the kernel
\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\ell}(s,x)
(resp.
\mathcal{P}^{R}(s,x)
) is left (resp. right) slice hyperholomorphic. Now, we prove the regularity of the kernel
\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\ell}(s,x)
(resp.
\mathcal{P}^{R}_{\ell}(s,x)
) in the second variable. By Proposition
6.11
we know that the
F
-kernel
F_{n}^{L}(s,x)
(resp.
F_{n}^{R}(s,x)
) is of axial type. Thus it is clear that also the function
\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\ell}(s,x)
(resp.
\mathcal{P}^{R}_{\ell}(s,x)
) is of axial type. The fact that
\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\ell}(s,x)
(resp.
\mathcal{P}^{R}_{\ell}(s,x)
) is polyanalytic in the variable
x
follows by Theorem
8.3
.
∎

Remark 8.13
.

One can prove that the kernel
\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\ell}(s,x)
(resp.
\mathcal{P}^{R}_{\ell}(s,x)
) is left (resp. right) axially polyanalytic of order
h_{n}-\ell+1
by using the so-called polyanalytic decomposition.
Since the kernel
F_{n}^{L}(s,x)
is left axially monogenic in the variable
x
, see Proposition
3.16
, and by Theorem
8.2
we get that
\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\ell}(s,x)
(resp.
\mathcal{P}^{R}_{\ell}(s,x)
) is left (resp. right) axially polyanalytic of order
h_{n}-\ell+1
in
x
.

Now, by using the series expansion of the
F
-kernel it is possible to write an expansion in series of the axially polyanalytic kernels.

Theorem 8.14
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
assume that
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
is such that
0\leq\ell\leq h_{n}-1
.
For
s
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
such that
|x|<|s|
we have

(8.7)

\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\ell}(s,x)=\frac{\gamma_{n}(-1)^{h_{n}-\ell}}{(h_{n}-\ell)!}\sum_{k=h_{n}+\ell}^{\infty}\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\tilde{k}_{h_{n},\ell,k,i}x_{0}^{i}P_{k-i-\ell-h_{n}}^{n}(x)s^{-1-k},

and

(8.8)

\mathcal{P}^{R}_{\ell}(s,x)=\frac{\gamma_{n}(-1)^{h_{n}-\ell}}{(h_{n}-\ell)!}\sum_{k=h_{n}+\ell}^{\infty}\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\tilde{k}_{h_{n},\ell,k,i}s^{-1-k}x_{0}^{i}P_{k-i-\ell-h_{n}}^{n}(x),

where

(8.9)

\tilde{k}_{h_{n},\ell,k,i}:=\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{i}\binom{k-i-\ell+h_{n}}{k-i-\ell-h_{n}}(-1)^{i+h_{n}-\ell},

and the homogeneous polynomials
P_{k}^{n}(x)
are defined in (
3.4
).

Proof.

By Theorem
8.10
, the expansion in series of the
F
-kernel, see Proposition
3.17
, and the Newton binomial we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\ell}(s,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}S^{-1}_{L}(s,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{\ell-h_{n}}}{(h_{n}-\ell)!}F_{n}^{L}(s,x)(s-x_{0})^{h_{n}-\ell}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{\ell-h_{n}}}{(h_{n}-\ell)!}\sum_{k=2h_{n}}^{\infty}(\Delta^{h_{n}}_{n+1}x^{k})s^{-1-k}(s-x_{0})^{h_{n}-\ell}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{h_{n}-\ell}}{(h_{n}-\ell)!}\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\sum_{k=2h_{n}}^{\infty}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{i}(\Delta^{h_{n}}_{n+1}x^{k})x_{0}^{i}(-1)^{i}s^{-1-k-\ell-i+h_{n}}.

By making the following change of index
k=u-\ell-i+h_{n}
we get

\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\ell}(s,x)=\frac{(-1)^{h_{n}-\ell}}{(h_{n}-\ell)!}\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\sum_{u=h_{n}+\ell+i}^{\infty}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{i}x_{0}^{i}(-1)^{i}\left(\Delta^{h_{n}}_{n+1}x^{u-i-\ell+h_{n}}\right)s^{-1-u}.

Now, we observe that if
h_{n}+\ell\leq u<h_{n}+\ell+i
then
\Delta^{h_{n}}_{n+1}(x^{u-i-\ell+h_{n}})=0
due to the fact that
u-i-\ell+h_{n}<2h_{n}
. Thus we have

\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\ell}(s,x)=\frac{(-1)^{h_{n}-\ell}}{(h_{n}-\ell)!}\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\sum_{u=h_{n}+\ell}^{\infty}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{i}x_{0}^{i}(-1)^{i}\left(\Delta^{h_{n}}_{n+1}x^{u-i-\ell+h_{n}}\right)s^{-1-u},

where we recall the relation
\Delta_{n+1}^{h_{n}}x^{m}=\gamma_{n}\binom{m}{m-2h_{n}}P^{n}_{m-2h_{n}}(x)
, for
m\geq 2h_{n}
is given in (
3.16
).
∎

Corollary 8.15
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
assume that
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
is such that
0\leq\ell\leq h_{n}-1
.
Then, for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
and for
k\geq 1
, we have

(8.10)

\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}x^{k}=\frac{\gamma_{n}(-1)^{h_{n}-\ell}}{(h_{n}-\ell)!}\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\tilde{k}_{h_{n},\ell,k,i}x_{0}^{i}P_{k-i-\ell-h_{n}}^{n}(x)

where
\tilde{k}_{h_{n},\ell,k,i}
are defined in (
8.9
),
\gamma_{n}
are defined in (
3.12
), and the homogeneous polynomials
P_{k}^{n}(x)
are defined in (
3.4
).

Proof.

We consider
s\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
such that
s\notin[x]
. By Proposition
2.9
, for
|x|<|s|
we have

(8.11)

\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}S^{-1}_{L}(s,x)=\sum_{k=h_{n}+\ell}^{\infty}\left(\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}x^{k}\right)s^{-1-k}.

Now, equalizing (
8.11
) and (
8.7
) and we obtain

\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}(x^{k})=\frac{(-1)^{\ell-h_{n}}}{(h_{n}-\ell)!}\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{i}x_{0}^{h_{n}-\ell-i}(-1)^{i}\left(\Delta^{h_{n}}_{n+1}x^{k+i}\right).

∎

Remark 8.16
.

If we consider
n=3
and
\ell=0
in (
8.10
) we obtain the same result as in
[
59
]
. If we consider
n=5
with
\ell=0
, and
n=5
with
\ell=1
, we reobtain the same result proved in
[
33
]
.

Now, we provide a characterization of the kernel of the operator
\Delta_{n+1}^{\ell}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}
but, before, we need the following technical lemma, whose proof requires the use of Jacobi polynomials.

Lemma 8.17
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
assume that
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
is such that
0\leq\ell\leq h_{n}-1
. Then we have

\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\tilde{k}_{h_{n},\ell,k,i}=\begin{cases}(-1)^{h_{n}-\ell}\frac{(h_{n}-\ell)!k!}{(k-h_{n}-\ell)!(2h_{n})!}\binom{2h_{n}}{h_{n}-\ell},\qquad k\geq 2h_{n}\\
\newline
(-1)^{h_{n}-\ell}\binom{k}{k-h_{n}-\ell},\qquad h_{n}+\ell\leq k<2h_{n}\end{cases}

where the constant
\tilde{k}_{h_{n},\ell,k,i}
is given in (
8.9
) and we used the convention that
\binom{\gamma}{\delta}=0
if
\gamma<\delta
.

Proof.

We start by changing indexes in the sum
u=-i+h_{n}-\ell
, and we get

\begin{split}\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\tilde{k}_{h_{n},\ell,k,i}&=\sum_{u=h_{n}-\ell}^{0}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{h_{n}-\ell-u}\binom{k+u}{k+u-2h_{n}}(-1)^{-u+2(h_{n}-\ell)}\\
&=\sum_{u=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{u}\binom{k+u}{k+u-2h_{n}}(-1)^{u}.\end{split}

For the sake of the simplicity we call the index in the last sum with letter
i
. Thus we want to show that for
k\geq 2h_{n}
we have

\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{i}\binom{k+i}{k+i-2h_{n}}(-1)^{i}=(-1)^{h_{n}-\ell}\frac{(h_{n}-\ell)!k!}{(k-h_{n}-\ell)!(2h_{n})!}\binom{2h_{n}}{h_{n}-\ell},

and that, for
h_{n}+\ell\leq k<2h_{n}
, we have

(8.12)

\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{i}\binom{k+i}{k+i-2h_{n}}(-1)^{i}=(-1)^{h_{n}-\ell}\binom{k}{k-h_{n}-\ell},

First we consider the case
k\geq 2h_{n}
. For the proof we need the properties of the Jacobi polynomials (see
[
86
]
, Chapter 4). We recall that the Jacobi polynomials for
\alpha,\,\beta,n\in\mathbb{N}
and
z\in\mathbb{C}
, are defined as

(8.13)

P^{(\alpha,\beta)}_{n}(z):=\frac{\Gamma(n+\alpha+1)}{n!\Gamma(n+\alpha+\beta+1)}\sum_{\nu=0}^{n}\binom{n}{\nu}\frac{\Gamma(n+\alpha+\beta+\nu+1)}{\Gamma(\alpha+\nu+1)}\left(\frac{z-1}{2}\right)^{\nu}.

For the Jacobi polynomials the following formula

(8.14)

P_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)}(-1)=(-1)^{n}\binom{n+\beta}{n},

holds (see formula (4.1.4) in
[
86
]
). We observe that by considering
n:=h_{n}-\ell
,
\alpha:=k-2h_{n}
and
\beta:=h_{n}+\ell
we get

\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{i}\binom{k+i}{k+i-2h}(-1)^{i}=\frac{(h_{n}-\ell)!k!}{(k-h_{n}-\ell)(2h)!}P^{(k-2h_{n},h_{n}+\ell)}_{h_{n}-\ell}(-1)

which, together with (
8.14
), implies that

\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{i}\binom{k+i}{k+i-2h_{n}}(-1)^{i}=(-1)^{h_{n}-\ell}\frac{(h_{n}-\ell)!k!}{(k-h_{n}-\ell)!(2h_{n})!}\binom{2h_{n}}{h_{n}-\ell}.

For the second case we have to consider
h_{n}+\ell\leq k<2h_{n}
. Since we have zero terms in the series (
8.12
) if
i<2h_{n}-k
we have

\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{i}\binom{k+i}{2h_{n}}(-1)^{i}=\sum_{i=2h_{n}-k}^{h_{n}-\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{i}\binom{k+i}{2h_{n}}(-1)^{i}.

By performing the change of index
i=\gamma+2h_{n}-k
, the series on the right-hand side can be rewritten as:

\sum_{i=2h_{n}-k}^{h_{n}-\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{i}\binom{k+i}{2h_{n}}(-1)^{i}=\sum_{\gamma=0}^{k-h_{n}-\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{\gamma+2h_{n}-k}\binom{\gamma+2h_{n}}{2h_{n}}(-1)^{\gamma}.

By using the definition of the Jacobi polynomials, see (
8.13
), with
\alpha:=2h_{n}-k
,
\beta:=h_{n}+\ell
, and
n:=k-h_{n}-\ell
, we can rewrite the above expression on the right-hand side as:

\sum_{\gamma=0}^{k-h_{n}-\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{\gamma+2h_{n}-k}\binom{\gamma+2h_{n}}{2h_{n}}(-1)^{\gamma}=(-1)^{k}P^{(2h_{n}-k,h_{n}+\ell)}_{k-h_{n}-\ell}(-1).

By using the property (
8.14
) of the Jacobi polynomials we have

\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{i}\binom{k+i}{2h_{n}}(-1)^{i}=(-1)^{h_{n}-\ell}\binom{k}{k-h_{n}-\ell}.

∎

Lemma 8.18
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
. Let
f\in\mathcal{SH}_{L}(U)
(resp.
f\in\mathcal{SH}_{R}(U)
) and
U
be a connected slice Cauchy domain. Then
f
belongs to
\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}
if and only if
f(x)=\sum_{\nu=0}^{h_{n}+\ell-1}x^{\nu}\alpha_{\nu}
(resp.
f(x)=\sum_{\nu=0}^{h_{n}+\ell-1}\alpha_{\nu}x^{\nu}
) where
\{\alpha_{\nu}\}_{0\leq\nu\leq h_{n}+\ell-1}\mathbb{\subseteq}\mathbb{R}_{n}
.

Proof.

We prove the result in the case
f\in\mathcal{SH}_{L}(\Omega)
since the case
f\in\mathcal{SH}_{R}(\Omega)
follows by similar arguments. We proceed by double inclusion. It is obvious that
f(x)=\sum_{\nu=0}^{h_{n}+\ell-1}x^{\nu}\alpha_{\nu}
belongs to the kernel
\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}
. The other inclusion can be proved observing that the function
f
belongs to
\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}
, after a change of variable if needed, there exists
r>0
such that the function
f
can be expanded in a convergent series at the origin

f(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}x^{k}\alpha_{k}\quad\textrm{for $\{\alpha_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\subset\mathbb{R}_{n}$ and for any $x\in B_{r}(0)$}

where
B_{r}(0)
is the ball centred at
0
and of radius
r
. We apply the operator
\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}
to the function
f
and we get

0=\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}f(x)\equiv\sum_{k=h_{n}+\ell}^{\infty}\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}(x^{k})\alpha_{k},\quad\forall x\in B_{r}(0).

We restrict
\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}(x^{k})
to a neighbourhood of the real axisis
\Omega
. Thus by Corollary
8.15
we have that

\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}(x^{k})|_{\underline{x}=0}=\left(\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\tilde{k}_{h_{n},\ell,k,i}\right)x_{0}^{k-h_{n}-\ell},

where by Lemma
8.17
we know that
\sum_{i=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\tilde{k}_{h_{n},\ell,k,i}
is different from zero. Thus we have that

\alpha_{k}=0,\qquad k\geq h_{n}+\ell.

This implies that
f(x)=\sum_{\nu=0}^{h_{n}+\ell-1}x^{\nu}\alpha_{\nu}
in
\Omega
, and since
U
is a connected set we get that
f(x)=\sum_{\nu=0}^{h_{n}+\ell-1}x^{\nu}\alpha_{\nu}
for any
x\in U
.
∎

Finally, we have all the necessary tools to provide an integral representation of a axially polyanalytic functions.

Theorem 8.19

(Integral representation of axially polyanalytic functions)
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
assume that
\ell
is such that
0\leq\ell\leq h_{n}-1
.
Let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be a bounded slice Cauchy domain, for
I\in\mathbb{S}
, we set
ds_{I}=ds(-I)
.

•

If
f
is a left slice hyperholomorphic function on a set that contains
\overline{U}
, then
\breve{f}^{\circ}(x)=\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}f(x)
is axially polyanalytic of order
h_{n}-\ell+1
and it admits the integral representation

(8.15)

\breve{f}^{\circ}(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\ell}(s,x)ds_{I}f(s),\qquad\forall x\in U,

•

If
f
is a right slice hyperholomorphic function on a set that contains
\overline{U}
,
then
\breve{f}^{\circ}(x)=f(x)\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}
is axially polyanalytic of order
h_{n}-\ell+1
and it admits the integral representation

(8.16)

\breve{f}^{\circ}(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}f(s)ds_{I}\mathcal{P}^{R}_{\ell}(s,x),\qquad\forall x\in U.

Moreover, the integrals in (
8.15
) and (
8.16
) depend neither on
U
and nor on the imaginary unit
I\in\mathbb{S}
and are independent on the kernel of
\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}
.

Proof.

We show only formula (
8.15
). By Theorem
2.14
and Theorem
8.10
we have

\breve{f}^{\circ}(x)=\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}f(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}S^{-1}_{L}(s,x)ds_{I}f(s)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\ell}(s,x)ds_{I}f(s).

Similar arguments as Theorem
4.33
show the independence on the kernel of
\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}
.
∎

9.
Polyanalytic functional calculus based on the
S
-spectrum

In this section, we investigate a polyanalytic functional calculus based on the S-spectrum. The key element of this investigation is the integral representation of axially polyanalytic functions, see Theorem
8.19
.
The diagram below shows the polyanalytic fine structure. The focus of our investigation here is the central part of the diagram, i.e., the polyanalytic functional calculus:

\begin{CD}\mathcal{SH}_{L}(U)@>{\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}}>{}>\mathcal{AP}_{h_{n}-\ell+1}^{L}(U)@>{D^{h_{n}-\ell}}>{}>\mathcal{AM}_{L}(U)\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
S\text{-Functional calculus}\text{Polyanalytic Functional calculus }F\text{-Functional calculus}\\
\end{CD}

A similar diagram holds also for functions in
\mathcal{SH}_{R}(U)
.

Definition 9.1

(Polyanalytic resolvent operators)
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
assume that
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
is such that
0\leq\ell\leq h_{n}-1
.
Let
T\in\mathcal{BC}^{0,1}(V_{n})
and
s\in\sigma_{S}(T)
. We define the left
\mathcal{P}
-resolvent operator as

\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\ell}(s,T)=\frac{(-1)^{h_{n}-\ell}}{(h_{n}-\ell)!}F_{n}^{L}(s,T)(s\mathcal{I}-T_{0})^{h_{n}-\ell},

and the right
\mathcal{P}
-resolvent operator as

\mathcal{P}^{R}_{\ell}(s,T)=\frac{(-1)^{h_{n}-\ell}}{(h_{n}-\ell)!}(s\mathcal{I}-T_{0})^{h_{n}-\ell}F_{n}^{R}(s,T),

where the
F
-resolvent operators
F_{n}^{L}(s,T)
and
F_{n}^{R}(s,T)
are defined in (
3.20
) and (
3.21
), respectively.

Now, we provide an expansion in series for the left and right
\mathcal{P}
-resolvent operators.

Proposition 9.2
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
assume that
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
is such that
0\leq\ell\leq h_{n}-1
.
Let
T\in\mathcal{BC}(V_{n})
and
s\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be such that
\|T\|<|s|
.
Then, we can write the left
\mathcal{P}
-resolvent operator as

(9.1)

\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\ell}(s,T)=\frac{\gamma_{n}}{(h_{n}-\ell)!}\sum_{m=h_{n}+\ell}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\tilde{k}_{h_{n},\ell,k,m}T_{0}^{k}P^{n}_{m-h_{n}-\ell-k}(T)s^{-1-m},

and the right
\mathcal{P}
-resolvent operator as

(9.2)

\mathcal{P}^{R}_{\ell}(s,T)=\frac{\gamma_{n}}{(h_{n}-\ell)!}\sum_{m=h_{n}+\ell}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\tilde{k}_{h_{n},\ell,k,m}s^{-1-m}T_{0}^{k}P^{n}_{m-h_{n}-\ell-k}(T),

where the constant
\tilde{k}_{h_{n},\ell,k,m}
is given in (
8.9
),
\gamma_{n}
is defined in
3.12
and
the Clifford-Appell operators
P_{k}^{n}(T)
are defined in (
3.19
).

Proof.

By using the expansion in series of the
F
-resolvent operator, see Proposition
3.24
, and by the binomial formula for
(s-T_{0})^{h_{n}-\ell}
, since
T_{0}
commute with
s
, we have that

\begin{split}\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\ell}(s,T)&=\frac{\gamma_{n}}{(h_{n}-\ell)!}\sum_{m=2h_{n}}^{\infty}\binom{m}{m-2h_{n}}P^{n}_{m-2h_{n}}(T)s^{-1-m}(s\mathcal{I}-T_{0})^{h_{n}-\ell}\\
&=\frac{\gamma_{n}}{(h_{n}-\ell)!}\sum_{m=2h_{n}}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{k}\binom{m}{m-2h_{n}}(-1)^{k}T_{0}^{k}P^{n}_{m-2h_{n}}(T)s^{-1-m+h_{n}-\ell-k}\\
&=\frac{\gamma_{n}}{(h_{n}-\ell)!}\sum_{u=h_{n}+\ell+k}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{k}\binom{u+h_{n}-\ell-k}{u-h_{n}-\ell-k}(-1)^{k}T_{0}^{k}P^{n}_{u-h_{n}-\ell-k}(T)s^{-1-u}.\end{split}

Now, if
h_{n}+\ell\leq u<h_{n}+\ell+k
we have
P^{n}_{u-h_{n}-\ell-k}(T)=0
since
u-h_{n}-\ell-k<0
, see Remark
3.21
. Thus we can write

\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\ell}(s,T)=\frac{\gamma_{n}}{(h_{n}-\ell)!}\sum_{u=h_{n}+\ell}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{k}\binom{u+h_{n}-\ell-k}{u-h_{n}-\ell-k}(-1)^{k}T_{0}^{k}P^{n}_{u-h_{n}-\ell-k}(T)s^{-1-u}.

The expansion for the right
\mathcal{P}
-resolvent operator follows by using similar arguments.
∎

Now, we show a result regarding the regularity of the left (resp. right)
\mathcal{P}
-resolvent operator.

Lemma 9.3
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
assume that
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
is such that
0\leq\ell\leq h_{n}-1
.
We also assume that
T\in\mathcal{BC}^{0,1}(V_{n})
. The polyanalytic left resolvent operator
\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\ell}(s,T)
(resp.
\mathcal{P}^{R}_{\ell}(s,T)
) is a
\mathcal{B}(V_{n})
-valued right (resp. left) slice hyperholomorphic function for all
s
in
\rho_{S}(T)
.

Proof.

To show the result it is sufficient to observe that
F_{n}^{L}(s,T)
(resp.
F_{n}^{R}(s,T)
) is a right (resp. left) slice hyperholomorphic in the variable
s
, see Lemma
5.7
, and
(T_{0}-s)^{h_{n}-\ell}
is an intrinsic function in the variable
s
operator-valued.
∎

Now, inspired from the integral representation of polyanalytic functions, see Theorem
8.19
we give the definition of polyanalytic functional calculus.

Remark 9.4
.

Because of the particular role that
T_{0}
plays in the expressions of the
\mathcal{P}
-resolvent operators, it is possible,
in the definition of the polyanalytic functional calculus to take advantage of
Remark
3.31
and consider
T\in\mathcal{BC}^{0,1}(V_{n})
such that its components
T_{i}
, for
i=0,1,...n-1
have real spectra and
T_{n}=0
.

Definition 9.5

(Polyanalytic functional calculus on the
S
-spectrum)
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
assume that
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
is such that
0\leq\ell\leq h_{n}-1
.
Let us consider
T\in\mathcal{BC}^{0,1}(V_{n})
be such that its components
T_{i}
, for
i=0,1,...n-1
have real spectra and
T_{n}=0
.
Let
U
be a bounded slice Cauchy domain and we set
ds_{I}=ds(-I)
for
I\in\mathbb{S}
.

•

For any
f\in\mathcal{SH}_{\sigma_{S}(T)}^{L}(U)
we set
\breve{f}^{\circ}(x)=\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}f
. Then we define the
\mathcal{P}
-functional calculus for the operator
T
as

(9.3)

\breve{f}^{\circ}(T):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\ell}(s,T)ds_{I}f(s),

•

For any
f\in\mathcal{SH}_{\sigma_{S}(T)}^{R}(U)
we set
\breve{f}^{\circ}(x)=\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}f
. Then we define the
\mathcal{P}
-functional calculus for the operator
T
as

(9.4)

\breve{f}^{\circ}(T):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}f(s)ds_{I}\mathcal{P}^{R}_{\ell}(s,T).

We now state and prove some results that show that the polyanalytic functional calculus is well defined.

Proposition 9.6
.

The polyanalytic functional calculus based on the
S
-spectrum is well defined, i.e. the integrals (
9.3
) and (
9.4
) depend neither on the imaginary units
I\in\mathbb{S}
nor on the slice Cauchy domain
U
.

Proof.

The result follows by employing arguments similar to those used in Theorem
5.10
. Specifically, in this case, the resolvent operator is left (respectively, right) slice hyperholomorphic in
s
, as shown in Lemma
7.4
. Furthermore,
\lim_{s\to\infty}\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\ell}(s,T)=\lim_{s\to\infty}\mathcal{P}^{R}_{\ell}(s,T)=0
.

∎

Due to the lack of commutativity, the right and left variants of the polyanalytic functional calculus often lead to different operators. The only exception occurs when the polyanalytic functional calculus is utilized for intrinsic slice hyperholomorphic functions.

Proposition 9.7
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
assume that
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
is such that
0\leq\ell\leq h_{n}-1
. We assume that
T\in\mathcal{BC}^{0,1}(V_{n})
and
f\in\mathcal{N}_{\sigma_{S}(T)}(U)
, where
U
is a bounded slice Cauchy domain as in
Definition
2.22
. Then we have

\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\ell}(s,T)\,ds_{I}\,f(s)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}f(s)ds_{I}\mathcal{P}^{R}_{\ell}(s,T).

Proof.

The result follows by using Theorem
3.27
.
∎

The following technical tool is fundamental to show the independence of the polyanalytic functional calculus from the kernel of
\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}
.

Theorem 9.8
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
assume that
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
is such that
0\leq\ell\leq h_{n}-1
.
Let us consider
T\in\mathcal{BC}^{0,1}(V_{n})
be such that its components
T_{i}
,
i=0,1,...,n-1
have real spectra and
T_{n}=0
.
Let
G
be a bounded slice Cauchy domain such that
(\partial G)\cap\sigma_{S}(T)=\emptyset
.
Thus, for every
I\in\mathbb{S}
and
1\leq\ell\leq h_{n}-1
,
we have

(9.5)

\int_{\partial(G\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}s^{\alpha}ds_{I}\mathcal{P}^{R}_{\ell}(s,T)=\int_{\partial(G\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\ell}(s,T)ds_{I}s^{\alpha}=0,\qquad\hbox{if}\quad\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\quad\hbox{with}\quad 0\leq\alpha\leq h_{n}+\ell-1.

Proof.

The first equality in (
9.5
) follows form the fact that the function
s^{\alpha}
is slice intrinsic holomorphic, see Proposition
9.7
. Now, we prove the second equality in (
9.5
). By the Fueter-Sce mapping theorem, see Theorem
3.19
, we have

(9.6)

\int_{\partial(G\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}F_{n}^{L}(s,x)ds_{I}s^{m}=\Delta^{h_{n}}_{n+1}(x^{m})=0,\qquad\hbox{if}\quad m\leq 2h_{n}-1,

for all
x\notin\partial G
and
I\in\mathbb{S}
. Now, by using the binomial theorem, the Fubini theorem and Proposition
3.42
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\partial(G\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\ell}(s,T)ds_{I}s^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(h_{n}-\ell)!}\sum_{k=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\int_{\partial(G\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{k}(-1)^{k}T_{0}^{h_{n}-\ell-k}F_{n}^{L}(s,T)ds_{I}s^{k+\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\partial W}G(\omega,T)D\omega\sum_{k=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\binom{h_{n}-\ell}{k}(-1)^{k}T_{0}^{h_{n}-\ell-k}\times

\displaystyle\times\left(\int_{\partial(G\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}F_{n}^{L}(s,\omega)ds_{I}s^{k+\alpha}\right),

where
W\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
as in Definition
3.33
. Since
k+\alpha\leq 2h_{n}-1
, by (
9.6
) we have

\int_{\partial(G\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\ell}(s,T)ds_{I}s^{\alpha}=0.

∎

Now, we show that the polyanalytic functional calculus is independent from the kernel of the operator
\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}
.

Proposition 9.9
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
assume that
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
is such that
0\leq\ell\leq h_{n}-1
.
Let us consider
T\in\mathcal{BC}^{0,1}(V_{n})
be such that its components
T_{i}
,
i=0,1,...n-1
have real spectra and
T_{n}=0
.
Let
U
be a slice Cauchy domain with
\sigma_{S}(T)\subset U
. Let
f
,
g\in\mathcal{SH}_{L}(U)
(resp.
f
,
g\in\mathcal{SH}_{R}(U)
) and
\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}f(x)=\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}g(x)

(resp.
f(x)\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}=g(x)\Delta^{\ell}_{n+1}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}
). Then, we have
\breve{f}^{\circ}(T)=\breve{g}^{\circ}(T)
.

Proof.

We prove the result only for the left case since for the right case the result follows by similar arguments.
We start proving the result assuming that
U
is connected. By the definition of the
polyanalytic functional calculus based on the
S
-spectrum, see Definition
9.5
, we have

\breve{f}^{\circ}(T)-\breve{g}^{\circ}(T)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\ell}(s,T)ds_{I}(f(s)-g(s)).

By Lemma
8.18
we know that the resolvent operator
\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\ell}(s,T)
is
\mathcal{B}(V_{n})
-valued slice hyperholomorphic. This implies that we can change the integration to
B_{r}(0)\cap\mathbb{C}_{I}
with
r>0
such that
\|T\|<r
. By hypothesis we have that
f(s)-g(s)\in\hbox{ker}(\Delta_{n+1}^{\ell}\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell})
, thus by Lemma
8.18
we have

\displaystyle\breve{f}^{\circ}(T)-\breve{g}^{\circ}(T)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{\nu=0}^{h_{n}+\ell-1}\int_{\partial(U\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\ell}(s,T)ds_{I}s^{\nu}\alpha_{\nu}

and by Theorem
9.8
the previous integral is zero (since
\nu\leq h_{n}+\ell-1
). This implies that
\breve{f}^{\circ}(T)=\breve{g}^{\circ}(T).

Now we consider the case when the set
U
is not connected.
In this case we can write
U=\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{k}U_{\ell}
where
U_{\ell}
are the connected components of the set
U
. So by Lemma
8.18
, we have
f(s)-g(s)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\sum_{\nu=0}^{h_{n}+\ell-1}\chi_{U_{\ell}}s^{\nu}\alpha_{\nu,\ell}
. Hence by the definition of the polyanalytic functional calculus, see Definition
9.5
, we have

\breve{f}^{\circ}(T)-\breve{g}^{\circ}(T)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\sum_{\nu=0}^{h_{n}+\ell-1}\int_{\partial(U_{\ell}\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\ell}(T)ds_{I}s^{\nu}\alpha_{\nu,\ell}.

Finally, by Theorem
9.8
we get that
\breve{f}^{\circ}(T)=\breve{g}^{\circ}(T)
.
∎

Now, we show some algebraic properties of the polyanalytic functional calculus.

Lemma 9.10
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
assume that
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
is such that
0\leq\ell\leq h_{n}-1
.
Let us consider
T\in\mathcal{BC}^{0,1}(V_{n})
be such that its components
T_{i}
,
i=0,1,...n-1
have real spectra and
T_{n}=0
.
If
\breve{f}^{\circ}(x)=\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}\Delta_{n+1}^{\ell}f(x)
(resp.
\breve{f}^{\circ}(x)=f(x)\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}\Delta_{n+1}^{\ell}
) and
\breve{g}^{\circ}(x)=\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}\Delta_{n+1}^{\ell}g(x)

(resp.
\breve{g}^{\circ}(x)=g(x)\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}\Delta_{n+1}^{\ell}
) with
f
,
g\in\mathcal{SH}_{\sigma_{S}(T)}^{L}(U)
(resp.
\mathcal{SH}_{\sigma_{S}(T)}^{R}(U)
) and
a\in\mathbb{R}_{n}
, then

(\breve{f}^{\circ}a+\breve{g}^{\circ})(T)=\breve{f}^{\circ}(T)a+\breve{g}^{\circ}(T),\qquad(resp.\,\,(a\breve{f}^{\circ}+\breve{g}^{\circ})(T)=a\breve{f}^{\circ}(T)+\breve{g}^{\circ}(T)).

Proof.

The result follows from the linearity of the integral (
9.3
) (resp. (
9.4
)).
∎

Proposition 9.11
.

Let
n
be an odd number and set
h_{n}:=(n-1)/2
assume that
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
is such that
0\leq\ell\leq h_{n}-1
.
Let us consider
T\in\mathcal{BC}^{0,1}(V_{n})
be such that its components
T_{i}
,
i=0,1,...n-1
have real spectra and
T_{n}=0
.

•

Let
\breve{f}^{\circ}(x)=\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}\Delta_{n+1}^{\ell}f(x)
and assume that
f(x)=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}x^{m}a_{m}
with
a_{m}\in\mathbb{R}_{n}
converges in a ball
B_{r}(0)
with
\sigma_{S}(T)\subset B_{r}(0)
. Then, we have

\breve{f}^{\circ}(T)=\frac{\gamma_{n}}{(h_{n}-\ell)!}\sum_{m=h_{n}+\ell}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\tilde{k}_{h_{n},\ell,k,m}T_{0}^{k}P^{n}_{m-h_{n}-\ell-k}(T)a_{m}

•

Let
\breve{f}^{\circ}(x)=f(x)\overline{D}^{h_{n}-\ell}\Delta_{n+1}^{\ell}
and assume that
f(x)=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}a_{m}x^{m}
with
a_{m}\in\mathbb{R}_{n}
, converges in a ball
B_{r}(0)
with
\sigma_{S}(T)\subset B_{r}(0)
. Then, we have

\breve{f}^{\circ}(T)=\frac{\gamma_{n}}{(h_{n}-\ell)!}\sum_{m=h_{n}+\ell}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}a_{m}\tilde{k}_{h_{n},\ell,k,m}T_{0}^{k}P^{n}_{m-h_{n}-\ell-k}(T).

Proof.

We prove only the first statement, as the second one follows from similar arguments. We choose an imaginary unit
I\in\mathbb{S}
and a radius
0<R<r
such that
\sigma_{S}(T)\subset B_{R}(0)
. Thus, the series expansion of
f
converges uniformly on
\partial(B_{R}(0)\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})
, and therefore we have

\displaystyle\breve{f}^{\circ}(T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial(B_{R}(0)\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\ell}(s,T)ds_{I}\sum_{\tau=0}^{\infty}s^{\tau}a_{\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{\tau=0}^{\infty}\int_{\partial(B_{R}(0)\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\ell}(s,T)ds_{I}s^{\tau}a_{\tau}.

By using the series expansion of the resolvent operator
\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\ell}(s,T)
, obtained in Proposition
9.2
, we have

\breve{f}^{\circ}(T)=\frac{\gamma_{n}}{2\pi(h_{n}-\ell)!}\sum_{\tau=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=h_{n}+\ell}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\tilde{k}_{h_{n},\ell,k,m}T_{0}^{k}P^{n}_{m-h_{n}-\ell-k}(T)\int_{\partial(B_{R}(0)\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}s^{-1-m+\tau}a_{\tau}ds_{I}.

Now, by using the fact that

\int_{\partial(B_{R}(0)\cap\mathbb{C}_{I})}s^{-1-m+\tau}ds_{I}\begin{cases}2\pi,\qquad\hbox{if}\quad\tau=m\\
0,\qquad\hbox{if}\quad\tau\neq m,\end{cases}

we get

\breve{f}^{\circ}(T)=\frac{\gamma_{n}}{(h_{n}-\ell)!}\sum_{m=h_{n}+\ell}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{h_{n}-\ell}\tilde{k}_{h_{n},\ell,k,m}T_{0}^{k}P^{n}_{m-h_{n}-\ell-k}(T)a_{m}.

This shows the result.
∎

10.
Concluding remarks

Remark 10.1

(The quaternionic fine structures)
.

In this remark, we aim to illustrate the quaternionic fine structures of Dirac type. This is an important case that has been widely studied in the literature, particularly regarding its various aspects on the functional calculi, and serves as a guideline for fine structures in the Clifford setting.
We recall that fine structures in spectral theory on the
S
-spectrum consist of the set of function spaces and the associated functional calculi induced by all possible factorizations of the operator
T_{FS2}
in the Fueter-Sce extension theorem.
We will denote the set of quaternionic slice hyperholomorphic functions by
\mathcal{SH}(U)
and the class of axially Fueter regular functions on
U
by
\mathcal{AM}(U)
.

Let
\mathcal{O}(\Pi)
is the set of holomorphic functions defined on
\Pi\subseteq\mathbb{C}
and
U
is an axially symmetric set in quaternions
\mathbb{H}
induced by
\Pi
.
The Fueter extension theorem can be summarized in the following diagram:

(10.1)

\begin{CD}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}(\Pi)}@>{T_{F1}}>{}>{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{SH}(U)}@>{\ \ T_{F2}}>{}>{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{AM}(U)},\end{CD}

where the two maps
T_{F1}
and
T_{F2}
are explicit operators,
